    1|       |//! `#[tinybus::interface]` — the attribute that turns an `impl` block into a
    2|       |//! dispatchable bus interface.
    3|       |//!
    4|       |//! # What it generates, and why by hand rather than by trait
    5|       |//!
    6|       |//! `tinybus::service::Interface` has to be object-safe: one connection holds
    7|       |//! a heterogeneous list of interfaces behind `dyn`, so its `call` takes and
    8|       |//! returns `serde_json::Value`. Writing that dispatch by hand for every service
    9|       |//! means a `match` on a string, a positional deserialize, and a serialize —
   10|       |//! three places per method for a typo to hide, and a fourth if the member list
   11|       |//! for introspection drifts from the `match`. The macro derives all four from
   12|       |//! one typed signature, so they cannot disagree.
   13|       |//!
   14|       |//! ```text
   15|       |//! struct Voice { model: WhisperModel }
   16|       |//!
   17|       |//! #[tinybus::interface(name = "ai.tinyhumans.openhuman.Voice")]
   18|       |//! impl Voice {
   19|       |//!     async fn transcribe(&self, path: String) -> tinybus::Result<String> { … }
   20|       |//!     async fn languages(&self) -> tinybus::Result<Vec<String>> { … }
   21|       |//! }
   22|       |//! ```
   23|       |//!
   24|       |//! generates `Transcribe` and `Languages` as members, deserializing a
   25|       |//! positional JSON array into each method's parameters.
   26|       |//!
   27|       |//! # The conventions, stated once
   28|       |//!
   29|       |//! - **`snake_case` becomes `PascalCase`.** `get_balance` is `GetBalance` on
   30|       |//!   the wire. Rust code stays idiomatic Rust; the wire stays conventional for
   31|       |//!   a bus. Override with `#[tinybus(name = "…")]` on the method when a member
   32|       |//!   has to match an existing contract exactly.
   33|       |//! - **Methods must be `async fn` taking `&self`** and returning
   34|       |//!   `tinybus::Result<T>` where `T: Serialize`. Not a stylistic preference: a
   35|       |//!   `&mut self` method would need exclusive access on a shared `Arc<dyn
   36|       |//!   Interface>`, which would serialise every call to the service.
   37|       |//! - **`#[tinybus(skip)]`** leaves a method off the bus entirely — helpers on
   38|       |//!   the same `impl` block do not have to move elsewhere to stay private.
   39|       |//! - **Arguments are positional.** Parameter names are a detail of the Rust
   40|       |//!   signature and renaming one must not break a caller.
   41|       |
   42|       |use proc_macro::TokenStream;
   43|       |use quote::{format_ident, quote};
   44|       |use syn::{
   45|       |    Error, FnArg, ImplItem, ItemImpl, LitStr, Meta, Pat, ReturnType, parse_macro_input,
   46|       |    punctuated::Punctuated, token::Comma,
   47|       |};
   48|       |

    1|       |//! The `tinybus` CLI: run the broker, and poke at a running one.
    2|       |//!
    3|       |//! Every subcommand is declared even where its milestone has not landed, so
    4|       |//! runbooks and scripts can be written against a stable surface and an
    5|       |//! unimplemented one fails with a message naming the milestone rather than
    6|       |//! "unknown subcommand".
    7|       |//!
    8|       |//! The important one is `monitor`. A bus whose traffic you cannot watch is a
    9|       |//! bus you debug by adding logging to two processes and restarting both; with
   10|       |//! it, "did the kernel actually call the wallet" is one command.
   11|       |
   12|       |use std::path::PathBuf;
   13|       |use std::time::Duration;
   14|       |
   15|       |use clap::{Parser, Subcommand};
   16|       |use tinybus::broker::Broker;
   17|       |use tinybus::connection::Connection;
   18|       |use tinybus::message::MessageKind;
   19|       |use tinybus::router::MatchRule;
   20|       |use tinybus::transport::unix::{UnixListenerAdapter, UnixTransport};
   21|       |use tinybus::{Error, Result};
   22|       |
   23|       |#[derive(Parser)]
   24|       |#[command(name = "tinybus", version, about, long_about = None)]
   25|       |struct Cli {
   26|       |    /// Path to the bus socket.
   27|       |    #[arg(long, env = tinybus::DEFAULT_SOCKET_ENV, global = true)]
   28|       |    address: Option<PathBuf>,
   29|       |
   30|       |    /// Seconds to wait for a reply before giving up.
   31|       |    #[arg(long, default_value_t = 30, global = true)]
   32|       |    timeout: u64,
   33|       |
   34|       |    #[command(subcommand)]
   35|       |    command: Command,
   36|       |}
   37|       |
   38|       |#[derive(Subcommand)]
   39|       |enum Command {
   40|       |    /// Run the broker until interrupted.
   41|       |    Serve,
   42|       |
   43|       |    /// Call a method and print the reply as JSON.
   44|       |    Call {
   45|       |        /// The destination name, e.g. `ai.tinyhumans.openhuman.Voice`.
   46|       |        destination: String,
   47|       |        /// The object path.
   48|       |        path: String,
   49|       |        /// The interface name.
   50|       |        interface: String,
   51|       |        /// The member to call.
   52|       |        member: String,
   53|       |        /// Positional arguments as a JSON array. Defaults to `[]`.
   54|       |        #[arg(default_value = "[]")]
   55|       |        args: String,
   56|       |    },
   57|       |
   58|       |    /// Emit a signal.
   59|       |    Emit {
   60|       |        /// The object path the signal comes from.
   61|       |        path: String,
   62|       |        /// The interface name.
   63|       |        interface: String,
   64|       |        /// The member to emit.
   65|       |        member: String,
   66|       |        /// The body as a JSON array. Defaults to `[]`.
   67|       |        #[arg(default_value = "[]")]
   68|       |        args: String,
   69|       |    },
   70|       |
   71|       |    /// List every name currently owned on the bus.
   72|       |    List,
   73|       |
   74|       |    /// Print every message matching a rule, until interrupted.
   75|       |    Monitor {
   76|       |        /// A match rule, e.g. `type=signal,interface=ai.tinyhumans.openhuman.Mail`.
   77|       |        #[arg(default_value = "type=signal")]
   78|       |        rule: String,
   79|       |    },
   80|       |
   81|       |    /// Check that the bus is reachable and report what is on it.
   82|       |    Doctor,
   83|       |}

/home/enamakel/work/tinybus/worktrees/zbus-coverage-90/crates/tinybus/src/broker.rs:
    1|       |//! The broker: accepts peers, owns the routing table, answers the bus's own
    2|       |//! interface.
    3|       |//!
    4|       |//! # Shape
    5|       |//!
    6|       |//! One accept loop, and per peer: a reader task and a writer task with a
    7|       |//! bounded queue between them. The queue is what makes a slow peer *its own*
    8|       |//! problem — a service that stops reading fills its queue, and senders to it
    9|       |//! block or fail, but no other peer's traffic is delayed. A single shared
   10|       |//! outbound path would let one wedged integration stall the bus, which is
   11|       |//! precisely the failure mode we are moving integrations out of the kernel to
   12|       |//! avoid.
   13|       |//!
   14|       |//! # What the broker does not do
   15|       |//!
   16|       |//! It does not start services, restart them, or know what any of them are for.
   17|       |//! Activation — "call the wallet, and if nothing owns that name, launch it" —
   18|       |//! is a real feature and a deliberate omission at this milestone; see
   19|       |//! `ROADMAP.md`. The broker also does not inspect bodies. It reads the header,
   20|       |//! routes, and forwards; a body is opaque bytes to it, which is what lets a
   21|       |//! service pass credentials over the bus without them being logged, cached, or
   22|       |//! parsed by a process that has no business seeing them.
   23|       |
   24|       |use std::sync::{Arc, Mutex};
   25|       |
   26|       |use serde_json::Value;
   27|       |use tokio::sync::mpsc;
   28|       |
   29|       |use crate::error::{Error, Result};
   30|       |use crate::message::{Message, MessageKind};
   31|       |use crate::name::{BusName, InterfaceName, MemberName, ObjectPath};
   32|       |use crate::ports::{Listener, Transport};
   33|       |use crate::router::{MatchRule, NameChange, Router};

  705|       |        // The load-bearing property of the whole design: a service that stops
  706|       |        // reading is isolated behind its own bounded queue.
  707|      1|        let (bus, _service, client) = bus().await;
  708|       |
  709|       |        // A peer that subscribes to everything and then never reads again —
  710|       |        // an integration blocked inside a third-party library, in other words.
  711|       |        // Driven at the transport level because a `Connection` would read the
  712|       |        // replies, which is the thing this peer is refusing to do.
  713|      1|        let sulker = bus.connect().await.unwrap();
  714|      1|        let mut subscribe = Message::method_call(
  715|      1|            BusName::new(crate::BUS_NAME).unwrap(),
  716|      1|            ObjectPath::new(crate::BUS_PATH).unwrap(),
  717|      1|            InterfaceName::new(crate::BUS_INTERFACE).unwrap(),
  718|      1|            MemberName::new("AddMatch").unwrap(),
  719|      1|            serde_json::json!(["type=signal"]),

  721|      1|        subscribe.header.serial = 1;
  722|      1|        sulker.send(subscribe).await.unwrap();
  723|       |
  724|      1|        for _ in 0..(PEER_QUEUE_CAPACITY * 2) {
  725|    512|            client

  733|    512|                .unwrap();

/home/enamakel/work/tinybus/worktrees/zbus-coverage-90/crates/tinybus/src/connection.rs:
    1|       |//! A peer's link to the broker: outgoing calls, incoming dispatch, signals.
    2|       |//!
    3|       |//! One [`Connection`] serves both roles a peer can have. The kernel uses the
    4|       |//! client half ([`Connection::call`], [`Connection::add_match`]); an
    5|       |//! integration additionally uses the service half ([`Connection::serve_at`],
    6|       |//! [`Connection::emit`]). They are not separate types because a service that
    7|       |//! calls another service is normal — the transcription service asking the
    8|       |//! notification service to say it is done should not need a second socket.
    9|       |//!
   10|       |//! # The dispatch loop
   11|       |//!
   12|       |//! Exactly one task reads the transport. It never awaits user code inline: a
   13|       |//! method call is handed to a spawned task, so a slow `Transcribe` cannot stop
   14|       |//! the connection from noticing that a reply to an earlier call has arrived.
   15|       |//! That is the whole reason calls carry serials rather than relying on order.
   16|       |//!
   17|       |//! # Timeouts
   18|       |//!
   19|       |//! Every call has a deadline, defaulting to [`DEFAULT_TIMEOUT`]. This is not
   20|       |//! optional and cannot be disabled, because the failure it prevents is the one
   21|       |//! that motivated the project: an integration wedged inside a third-party
   22|       |//! library used to wedge the kernel with it. A timeout does *not* cancel the
   23|       |//! remote work — tinybus cannot — it stops waiting and frees the caller.
   24|       |
   25|       |use std::collections::HashMap;
   26|       |use std::sync::Arc;
   27|       |use std::sync::atomic::{AtomicU64, Ordering};
   28|       |use std::time::Duration;
   29|       |
   30|       |use serde::Serialize;
   31|       |use serde::de::DeserializeOwned;
   32|       |use serde_json::Value;
   33|       |use tokio::sync::{Mutex, RwLock, broadcast, mpsc, oneshot};
   34|       |
   35|       |use crate::error::{Error, Result};
   36|       |use crate::message::{Header, Message, MessageKind};
   37|       |use crate::name::{BusName, InterfaceName, MemberName, ObjectPath};
   38|       |use crate::ports::Transport;
   39|       |use crate::proxy::Proxy;
   40|       |use crate::router::MatchRule;
   41|       |use crate::service::{Interface, ObjectTree};
   42|       |use crate::version::{Compatibility, PeerManifest, PeerRecord};
   43|       |
   44|       |/// How long a call waits before giving up.
   45|       |///
   46|       |/// Thirty seconds is long enough for a model round-trip or a cold service
   47|       |/// start, and short enough that a wedged integration surfaces as an error
   48|       |/// inside one user interaction rather than as a hang.
   49|       |pub const DEFAULT_TIMEOUT: Duration = Duration::from_secs(30);
   50|       |
   51|       |/// How many signals may queue for a subscriber before the oldest are dropped.
   52|       |///
   53|       |/// Dropping rather than blocking is the right trade for a broadcast: one slow
   54|       |/// subscriber must not stall every other subscriber, and a lagging receiver is
   55|       |/// told it lagged (`RecvError::Lagged`) rather than being silently starved.
   56|       |pub const SIGNAL_BUFFER: usize = 256;
   57|       |
   58|       |/// How many outbound messages may queue before a sender waits.
   59|       |///
   60|       |/// The queue is what makes [`Connection::try_send`] — and therefore a
   61|       |/// synchronous, fire-and-forget `publish` — possible at all: a sync caller
   62|       |/// cannot await a socket write, but it can hand a message to a writer task.
   63|       |/// Bounded, because an unbounded outbox turns a slow broker into unbounded
   64|       |/// growth in the process doing the publishing.
   65|       |pub const OUTBOX_CAPACITY: usize = 1024;
   66|       |
   67|       |struct Inner {
   68|       |    transport: Arc<dyn Transport>,
   69|       |    /// Everything outbound goes through here and out via the writer task.
   70|       |    /// Serialising writes through one task is also what lets `send` be called
   71|       |    /// concurrently without interleaving two frames on the wire.
   72|       |    outbox: mpsc::Sender<Message>,
   73|       |    serial: AtomicU64,
   74|       |    pending: Mutex<HashMap<u64, oneshot::Sender<Message>>>,
   75|       |    objects: RwLock<ObjectTree>,
   76|       |    /// A *std* lock, not a tokio one, so a synchronous publisher can stamp
   77|       |    /// the sender on a locally looped-back signal without an await.
   78|       |    unique_name: std::sync::RwLock<Option<BusName>>,
   79|       |    signals: broadcast::Sender<Message>,
   80|       |}
   81|       |
   82|       |/// Closes the transport when the last [`Connection`] handle goes away.
   83|       |///
   84|       |/// Needed because the dispatch task holds its own `Arc<Inner>`, so the
   85|       |/// refcount on `Inner` never reaches zero while that task lives — and the task
   86|       |/// only exits when the transport closes. Without this guard, dropping the last
   87|       |/// `Connection` would leak a task, and, worse, the *broker* would never see the
   88|       |/// hangup: a service that exited would keep its well-known name until the
   89|       |/// process died. `NameOwnerChanged` firing promptly is the whole reason the
   90|       |/// kernel can react to an integration dying instead of timing out on it.
   91|       |struct CloseOnDrop(Arc<Inner>);
   92|       |
   93|       |impl Drop for CloseOnDrop {
   94|     80|    fn drop(&mut self) {
   95|     80|        let inner = self.0.clone();
   96|       |        // Spawned, because `close` is async and `Drop` is not. If there is no
   97|       |        // runtime left to spawn on the process is going away anyway, which

  173|       |    /// Deliver a signal to *this* connection's own subscribers, without the
  174|       |    /// broker.
  175|       |    ///
  176|       |    /// The broker never echoes a signal back to the peer that sent it — that is
  177|       |    /// what stops a service which both emits and subscribes on one interface
  178|       |    /// from looping. But a host whose publishers and subscribers live in the
  179|       |    /// same process still expects its own subscribers to see what it published,
  180|       |    /// which was free when the bus was a `tokio::sync::broadcast` inside that
  181|       |    /// process.
  182|       |    ///
  183|       |    /// This closes that gap explicitly: the publisher loops the signal back
  184|       |    /// locally and the broker fans it out to everyone else, so every subscriber
  185|       |    /// anywhere sees it exactly once. It is a separate method rather than
  186|       |    /// behaviour folded into [`Connection::emit`] precisely because the
  187|       |    /// loop-prevention it steps around is load-bearing for services; the caller
  188|       |    /// has to mean it.

  192|       |        // the normal case for a write-only publisher.
  193|  2.06k|        let _ = self.inner.signals.send(message);
  194|  2.06k|    }

  445|       |    /// Send a message without awaiting, failing rather than blocking when the

  449|       |    /// domain that wants to announce "a message arrived" from a plain `fn`
  450|       |    /// cannot await a socket write, and making it await would push `async` up
  451|       |    /// through hundreds of call sites that have no other reason to have it.
  452|       |    ///
  453|       |    /// A full outbox means the process is producing faster than the broker is
  454|       |    /// draining. Returning [`Error::Backpressure`] rather than blocking is the
  455|       |    /// deliberate choice: a notification is worth less than the latency of the
  456|       |    /// caller that would be stalled to deliver it, and a caller that *does*
  457|       |    /// care can use [`Connection::send`].

  460|  2.06k|        message.validate()?;
                                        ^0

                                                                            ^1.02k
  462|  1.02k|            mpsc::error::TrySendError::Full(_) => Error::Backpressure,
  463|      0|            mpsc::error::TrySendError::Closed(_) => Error::ConnectionClosed,
  464|  1.02k|        })
  465|  2.06k|    }

/home/enamakel/work/tinybus/worktrees/zbus-coverage-90/crates/tinybus/src/error.rs:
    1|       |//! The crate error type and its `Result` alias.
    2|       |//!
    3|       |//! Every fallible function in tinybus returns [`Result<T>`]. Two things make
    4|       |//! this error type unusual, and both are deliberate:
    5|       |//!
    6|       |//! 1. It round-trips the wire. A method call that fails in a service process
    7|       |//!    has to arrive back at the caller as an error, not as a successful reply
    8|       |//!    containing a sad-looking value — so [`Error::MethodFailed`] carries a
    9|       |//!    stable `name` (`ai.tinyhumans.tinybus.Error.UnknownMethod`) that a caller
   10|       |//!    can match on across a process boundary, plus prose for humans.
   11|       |//! 2. It never carries the payload that caused it. Bodies routinely hold
   12|       |//!    OAuth tokens, mail contents and wallet material; a `Debug`-printed
   13|       |//!    message in a log is an exfiltration path. Errors name the *member* and
   14|       |//!    the *type*, never the value.
   15|       |
   16|       |use std::path::PathBuf;
   17|       |
   18|       |use crate::name::{BusName, InterfaceName, MemberName, ObjectPath};
   19|       |
   20|       |/// Errors produced anywhere in tinybus.
   21|       |#[derive(Debug, thiserror::Error)]
   22|       |pub enum Error {
   23|       |    /// A name, path, interface or member did not satisfy its grammar.
   24|       |    ///
   25|       |    /// Validation happens at the newtype boundary, so this is raised at
   26|       |    /// construction time rather than in the router — a malformed destination
   27|       |    /// can never reach the routing table.
   28|       |    #[error("invalid {kind} {input:?}: {reason}")]
   29|       |    InvalidName {
   30|       |        /// What was being parsed: `bus name`, `object path`, …
   31|       |        kind: &'static str,
   32|       |        /// The offending input, quoted.
   33|       |        input: String,
   34|       |        /// Which rule it broke.
   35|       |        reason: String,
   36|       |    },
   37|       |
   38|       |    /// The message could not be framed, parsed, or exceeded the size cap.
   39|       |    #[error("protocol: {0}")]
   40|       |    Protocol(String),
   41|       |
   42|       |    /// The transport is gone: the socket closed, the peer exited, the
   43|       |    /// in-memory channel was dropped.
   44|       |    #[error("transport: {0}")]
   45|       |    Transport(String),
   46|       |
   47|       |    /// The connection's dispatch loop has stopped, so no further calls can be
   48|       |    /// made on it. Terminal — reconnect rather than retry.
   49|       |    #[error("connection closed")]
   50|       |    ConnectionClosed,
   51|       |
   52|       |    /// The outbound queue is full: this process is producing faster than the
   53|       |    /// broker is draining.
   54|       |    ///
   55|       |    /// Only ever returned by the non-blocking senders
   56|       |    /// ([`crate::Connection::try_send`] and friends). It is a *dropped
   57|       |    /// notification*, not a lost call — a caller that cannot tolerate the drop
   58|       |    /// should use the awaiting send and take the backpressure instead.
   59|       |    #[error("outbound queue is full; message dropped")]
   60|       |    Backpressure,
   61|       |
   62|       |    /// No peer owns the destination name.
   63|       |    ///
   64|       |    /// The common cause is an integration that has not been started yet, which
   65|       |    /// is why the message names the destination rather than saying "not found".
   66|       |    #[error("no peer owns the name `{0}`")]
   67|       |    NameHasNoOwner(BusName),
   68|       |
   69|       |    /// `RequestName` lost: another peer already owns it and did not allow
   70|       |    /// replacement.
   71|       |    #[error("`{name}` is already owned by {owner}")]
   72|       |    NameTaken {
   73|       |        /// The contested well-known name.
   74|       |        name: BusName,
   75|       |        /// The unique name of the peer that holds it.
   76|       |        owner: BusName,
   77|       |    },
   78|       |
   79|       |    /// The destination peer exports no object at that path.
   80|       |    ///
   81|       |    /// Carries only the path: the caller already knows which destination it
   82|       |    /// addressed, and the *service* — which is where this is raised — knows
   83|       |    /// its unique name but not which of its names the caller used.
   84|       |    #[error("no object at `{path}`")]
   85|       |    UnknownObject {
   86|       |        /// The path that was asked for.
   87|       |        path: ObjectPath,
   88|       |    },
   89|       |
   90|       |    /// The object exists but does not implement that interface.
   91|       |    #[error("{path}: no interface `{interface}`")]
   92|       |    UnknownInterface {
   93|       |        /// The object that was found.
   94|       |        path: ObjectPath,
   95|       |        /// The interface that was not on it.
   96|       |        interface: InterfaceName,
   97|       |    },
   98|       |
   99|       |    /// The interface exists but has no such member.
  100|       |    #[error("{interface}: no member `{member}`")]
  101|       |    UnknownMethod {
  102|       |        /// The interface that was dispatched to.
  103|       |        interface: InterfaceName,
  104|       |        /// The member that was not on it.
  105|       |        member: MemberName,
  106|       |    },
  107|       |
  108|       |    /// An event's domain is not usable as an object-path element.
  109|       |    ///
  110|       |    /// A catalog bug in the host, not a bus failure: the event cannot be
  111|       |    /// addressed, so it cannot be published. Named rather than silently
  112|       |    /// dropped, because an event that vanishes with no error is the hardest
  113|       |    /// possible thing to debug from the subscriber's end.
  114|       |    #[error("event domain {domain:?} is not a usable path element: {reason}")]
  115|       |    InvalidDomain {
  116|       |        /// The offending domain string.
  117|       |        domain: String,
  118|       |        /// Why it cannot be a path element.
  119|       |        reason: String,
  120|       |    },
  121|       |
  122|       |    /// A method's arguments did not deserialize into the signature it declares.
  123|       |    ///
  124|       |    /// Carries the member and the serde message — never the arguments.
  125|       |    #[error("{member}: bad arguments: {reason}")]
  126|       |    BadArguments {
  127|       |        /// The member that was called.
  128|       |        member: MemberName,
  129|       |        /// What serde objected to.
  130|       |        reason: String,
  131|       |    },
  132|       |
  133|       |    /// A peer speaks a version of an interface this peer cannot work with.
  134|       |    ///
  135|       |    /// Raised by [`crate::Connection::require`] at the point of checking, not
  136|       |    /// at the point of failing — which is the difference between a startup
  137|       |    /// error naming two versions and a deserialize error hours later naming
  138|       |    /// neither.
  139|       |    #[error("{peer} is not compatible on {interface}: {detail}")]
  140|       |    IncompatibleVersion {
  141|       |        /// The peer that was checked.
  142|       |        peer: String,
  143|       |        /// The interface in question.
  144|       |        interface: String,
  145|       |        /// Which side rejected which version.
  146|       |        detail: String,
  147|       |    },
  148|       |
  149|       |    /// A method ran and failed. This is the variant that crosses the wire.
  150|       |    #[error("{name}: {message}")]
  151|       |    MethodFailed {
  152|       |        /// A stable, dotted error name a caller can match on.
  153|       |        name: String,
  154|       |        /// Prose for a human. Never the arguments, never a credential.
  155|       |        message: String,
  156|       |    },
  157|       |
  158|       |    /// A call exceeded its deadline. The service may still be running it;
  159|       |    /// tinybus does not cancel remote work, it stops waiting.
  160|       |    #[error("call to `{member}` timed out after {timeout_ms}ms")]
  161|       |    Timeout {
  162|       |        /// The member that was called.
  163|       |        member: MemberName,
  164|       |        /// The deadline that elapsed.
  165|       |        timeout_ms: u64,
  166|       |    },
  167|       |
  168|       |    /// A path that had to exist did not, or could not be used.
  169|       |    #[error("{path}: {message}")]
  170|       |    Path {
  171|       |        /// The offending path.
  172|       |        path: PathBuf,
  173|       |        /// What went wrong with it.
  174|       |        message: String,
  175|       |    },
  176|       |
  177|       |    /// A feature required for this code path was not compiled in.
  178|       |    #[error("{0} requires the `{1}` feature; rebuild with --features {1}")]
  179|       |    FeatureDisabled(&'static str, &'static str),
  180|       |
  181|       |    /// Filesystem or socket I/O failed.
  182|       |    #[error("io: {0}")]
  183|       |    Io(#[from] std::io::Error),
  184|       |
  185|       |    /// JSON serialization or deserialization failed.
  186|       |    #[error("json: {0}")]
  187|       |    Json(#[from] serde_json::Error),
  188|       |}

  289|       |/// Replace every backtick-quoted span with `…`.
  290|       |///
  291|       |/// serde puts the values it rejected in backticks, and so do most of the
  292|       |/// libraries a service will wrap. Redacting the span rather than dropping the
  293|       |/// whole message keeps the diagnostic — "invalid type: integer, expected a
  294|       |/// string" still tells you what went wrong — while making the error safe to log
  295|       |/// and safe to send to a peer that must not see the argument.

  297|      4|    let mut out = String::with_capacity(message.len());
  298|      4|    let mut inside = false;
  299|    142|    for c in message.chars() {
                           ^4      ^4
  300|    142|        match (c, inside) {
  301|      3|            ('`', false) => {
  302|      3|                out.push_str("`…");
  303|      3|                inside = true;
  304|      3|            }
  305|      2|            ('`', true) => {
  306|      2|                out.push('`');
  307|      2|                inside = false;
  308|      2|            }
  309|    122|            (_, false) => out.push(c),
  310|     15|            (_, true) => {}
  311|       |        }
  312|       |    }
  313|      4|    out
  314|      4|}

/home/enamakel/work/tinybus/worktrees/zbus-coverage-90/crates/tinybus/src/events/mod.rs:
    1|       |//! Typed pub/sub over the bus: publish a domain event, subscribe with a filter.
    2|       |//!
    3|       |//! This is OpenHuman's `core::event_bus` broadcast surface, ported onto tinybus
    4|       |//! and made generic over the event type. The API is deliberately the same shape
    5|       |//! — [`EventHandler`] with a `domains()` filter, an RAII
    6|       |//! [`SubscriptionHandle`], a synchronous fire-and-forget `publish` — because
    7|       |//! several hundred call sites depend on that shape, and because the shape was
    8|       |//! right. What changed underneath is the transport: events used to reach only
    9|       |//! subscribers inside one process, and now reach every peer on the bus.
   10|       |//!
   11|       |//! # Why generic over `E`
   12|       |//!
   13|       |//! The event catalog is the *host's* vocabulary. A bus crate that knew what
   14|       |//! `AgentTurnCompleted` meant would invert the dependency it exists to remove —
   15|       |//! and would have to be rebuilt every time a domain gains an event. So the host
   16|       |//! defines its enum, implements [`Event`], and keeps the catalog where the
   17|       |//! domains that emit it live.
   18|       |//!
   19|       |//! # How an event maps onto the wire
   20|       |//!
   21|       |//! | Event concept | Bus concept |
   22|       |//! | --- | --- |
   23|       |//! | the catalog | one interface, e.g. `ai.tinyhumans.openhuman.Events` |
   24|       |//! | `event.domain()` | the last element of the object path |
   25|       |//! | the event itself | the signal body |
   26|       |//!
   27|       |//! Putting the domain in the *path* rather than in the body is what lets the
   28|       |//! broker do the filtering: a peer that only cares about `cron` subscribes to
   29|       |//! `…/events/cron` and is never woken for anything else. Filtering in the
   30|       |//! subscriber — which is what the in-process bus had to do — means every
   31|       |//! process pays to deserialize and discard every event any domain emits.
   32|       |//!
   33|       |//! Subscribers still filter client-side as well, because every subscriber on
   34|       |//! one connection shares that connection's signal stream and has to demultiplex
   35|       |//! it. The two filters are not redundant: the broker-side one decides whether
   36|       |//! this *process* wakes up, the client-side one decides which handler runs.
   37|       |
   38|       |mod subscriber;
   39|       |

   58|       |/// filtering would be describing a domain that is too coarse.
   59|       |pub const PUBLISHED: &str = "Published";

  254|       |/// Why a non-blocking [`EventReceiver::try_recv`] produced nothing.
  255|       |#[derive(Debug, Clone, Copy, PartialEq, Eq)]
  256|       |pub enum TryRecvError {
  257|       |    /// Nothing is waiting right now.
  258|       |    Empty,
  259|       |    /// The receiver fell behind and `n` messages were dropped.
  260|       |    ///
  261|       |    /// Surfaced rather than hidden: a consumer draining for "did anything
  262|       |    /// change" must treat a lag as "yes, probably" and re-scan, because the
  263|       |    /// event it needed may have been one of the dropped ones.
  264|       |    Lagged(u64),
  265|       |    /// The connection is gone; nothing further will arrive.
  266|       |    Closed,
  267|       |}
  268|       |
  269|       |impl<E: Event> EventReceiver<E> {
  270|       |    /// Take the next event without waiting.
  271|       |    ///
  272|       |    /// For a consumer that drains on its own schedule — an agent turn checking
  273|       |    /// "did the installed tools change since last time" — rather than parking
  274|       |    /// a task on the stream. Skips messages belonging to other catalogs, so
  275|       |    /// `Empty` means "nothing *for you*", not "nothing at all".
  276|      5|    pub fn try_recv(&mut self) -> std::result::Result<E, TryRecvError> {
  277|       |        use tokio::sync::broadcast::error::TryRecvError as Raw;
  278|       |        loop {
  279|      6|            match self.signals.try_recv() {
  280|      3|                Ok(message) => {
  281|      3|                    if let Some(event) = decode(&self.config, &message) {
                                              ^2
  282|      2|                        return Ok(event);
  283|      1|                    }
  284|       |                }

    1|       |//! The subscriber side: the [`EventHandler`] trait and its RAII handle.
    2|       |//!
    3|       |//! Ported from OpenHuman's `core::event_bus::subscriber`, with the handler
    4|       |//! generic over the event type and the dispatch loop reading bus signals rather
    5|       |//! than a `tokio::sync::broadcast` of Rust values. Three behaviours are carried
    6|       |//! over deliberately, because each one exists to stop a specific failure:
    7|       |//!
    8|       |//! - **Domain filtering before dispatch**, so a handler that asked for `cron`
    9|       |//!   is not woken by every agent turn.
   10|       |//! - **Panic isolation**, so one handler panicking does not kill the loop and
   11|       |//!   silently unsubscribe every *other* handler on that connection.
   12|       |//! - **Lag is survivable**, so a subscriber that falls behind during a burst
   13|       |//!   logs and continues rather than terminating for good.
   14|       |
   15|       |use std::sync::Arc;
   16|       |
   17|       |use async_trait::async_trait;
   18|       |use tokio::sync::broadcast;
   19|       |use tokio::task::JoinHandle;
   20|       |
   21|       |use crate::events::{Event, EventBusConfig};
   22|       |use crate::message::Message;
   23|       |
   24|       |/// A typed event handler. Implement this to react to events.
   25|       |#[async_trait]
   26|       |pub trait EventHandler<E: Event>: Send + Sync + 'static {
   27|       |    /// Human-readable name, for logging and diagnostics.
   28|       |    fn name(&self) -> &str;
   29|       |
   30|       |    /// Optional domain filter. `None` receives everything in the catalog;
   31|       |    /// `Some(&["agent", "cron"])` receives only those domains.
   32|      2|    fn domains(&self) -> Option<&[&str]> {
   33|      2|        None
   34|      2|    }
   35|       |
   36|       |    /// Handle one event. Must not block the runtime.
   37|       |    async fn handle(&self, event: &E);
   38|       |}
   39|       |
   40|       |/// A running subscriber. Dropping it aborts the subscriber's task.
   41|       |///
   42|       |/// RAII rather than an explicit unsubscribe because the common bug it prevents
   43|       |/// is a subscriber outliving the thing it updates: a handler holding an `Arc`
   44|       |/// to state its owner has torn down keeps reacting to events forever.
   45|       |pub struct SubscriptionHandle {
   46|       |    task: JoinHandle<()>,
   47|       |    name: String,
   48|       |}
   49|       |
   50|       |impl SubscriptionHandle {
   51|     11|    pub(crate) fn new(name: String, task: JoinHandle<()>) -> Self {
   52|     11|        Self { task, name }
   53|     11|    }
   54|       |
   55|       |    /// The subscriber's name.
   56|      0|    pub fn name(&self) -> &str {
   57|      0|        &self.name
   58|      0|    }
   59|       |
   60|       |    /// Cancel the subscriber explicitly.
   61|      0|    pub fn cancel(self) {
   62|      0|        tracing::debug!(subscriber = self.name, "[tinybus] cancelling subscriber");
   63|      0|        self.task.abort();
   64|      0|    }
   65|       |
   66|       |    /// Leak the subscriber, so it runs for the life of the process.
   67|       |    ///
   68|       |    /// For subscribers registered at startup that genuinely should never stop.
   69|       |    /// Named `forget` rather than being the default because an accidentally
   70|       |    /// dropped handle is a subscriber that silently stops working, and that
   71|       |    /// should be a visible choice.
   72|      0|    pub fn forget(self) {
   73|      0|        std::mem::forget(self);
   74|      0|    }
   75|       |}
   76|       |

   78|     11|    fn drop(&mut self) {

   80|     11|            tracing::debug!(
   81|       |                subscriber = self.name,
   82|       |                "[tinybus] subscriber dropped, aborting task"
   83|       |            );
   84|     11|            self.task.abort();
   85|      0|        }
   86|     11|    }
   87|       |}

   89|       |/// Spawn the dispatch loop for one handler.
   90|     11|pub(crate) fn spawn<E: Event>(
   91|     11|    mut signals: broadcast::Receiver<Message>,
   92|     11|    config: EventBusConfig,
   93|     11|    handler: Arc<dyn EventHandler<E>>,

   95|     11|    let name = handler.name().to_string();
   96|       |    // Snapshot the filter as owned strings: `domains()` borrows from the
   97|       |    // handler, and the loop outlives the borrow.

   99|     11|        .domains()

                               ^1^1     ^1      ^1^1           ^1

  104|       |        domains = ?domains,

  106|       |    );

  110|       |        loop {

  120|       |                }

  126|      0|                    break;

  129|       |

  131|      0|                continue;
  132|       |            };
  133|       |
  134|     44|            if let Some(allowed) = &domains
                                      ^3
  135|      3|                && !allowed.iter().any(|d| d == event.domain())
  136|       |            {

  139|       |
  140|       |            // A panicking handler must not take this loop with it: losing the
  141|       |            // loop silently unsubscribes the handler, so it stops reacting and
  142|       |            // nothing says so.
  143|       |            //
  144|       |            // The isolation is a spawned task rather than `catch_unwind`,
  145|       |            // because tokio already turns a panicking task into an `Err` on its

/home/enamakel/work/tinybus/worktrees/zbus-coverage-90/crates/tinybus/src/global.rs:
    1|       |//! Process-wide singletons: one bus, one native registry, initialised once.
    2|       |//!
    3|       |//! A host application has exactly one bus, and every domain in it needs to
    4|       |//! reach that bus from code that was not handed a reference — a `publish` deep
    5|       |//! inside a scheduler, a handler registered from a `Once::call_once`. That is
    6|       |//! what this provides, and it is why OpenHuman's bus was a singleton before any
    7|       |//! of this existed.
    8|       |//!
    9|       |//! # Why the host declares the static, not tinybus
   10|       |//!
   11|       |//! The bus is generic over the host's event type, so tinybus cannot own a
   12|       |//! `static` of it — there is no single type to name. Instead the host writes:
   13|       |//!
   14|       |//! ```ignore
   15|       |//! static BUS: tinybus::global::OnceBus<DomainEvent> = tinybus::global::OnceBus::new();
   16|       |//! ```
   17|       |//!
   18|       |//! …and gets the whole surface off it. `OnceBus::new` is `const`, so this costs
   19|       |//! nothing until something initialises it.
   20|       |//!
   21|       |//! # Before initialisation
   22|       |//!
   23|       |//! Every accessor is safe to call before `init`. Publishing goes nowhere and
   24|       |//! logs at `trace`; subscribing returns `None`. This is deliberate and carried
   25|       |//! over from the bus being replaced: a domain that publishes during early
   26|       |//! startup, or inside a unit test that never stood a bus up, must not panic.
   27|       |//! The cost is that a genuinely missing `init` is quiet — which is what
   28|       |//! [`OnceBus::is_initialised`] and the startup log line are for.
   29|       |
   30|       |use std::sync::Arc;
   31|       |use std::sync::OnceLock;
   32|       |
   33|       |use crate::broker::Broker;
   34|       |use crate::connection::Connection;
   35|       |use crate::error::Result;
   36|       |use crate::events::{Event, EventBus, EventBusConfig, EventHandler, SubscriptionHandle};
   37|       |use crate::native::NativeRegistry;
   38|       |use crate::ports::Transport;
   39|       |use crate::transport::memory::MemoryBus;
   40|       |use crate::version::PeerManifest;
   41|       |

  103|       |        // the one everyone gets, and ours is dropped.

/home/enamakel/work/tinybus/worktrees/zbus-coverage-90/crates/tinybus/src/lib.rs:
    1|       |//! tinybus — a zbus-style message bus for everything the OpenHuman kernel talks
    2|       |//! to that is not the OpenHuman kernel.
    3|       |//!
    4|       |//! # Why this exists
    5|       |//!
    6|       |//! The kernel's dependency graph grew by absorption. A speech feature pulled in
    7|       |//! `whisper-rs` and `cpal`; a document tool pulled in `pdf-extract`, `ppt-rs`
    8|       |//! and `docx-rs`; a wallet pulled in `ethers`, `bitcoin` and
    9|       |//! `curve25519-dalek`; a browser tool pulled in `fantoccini`. None of that is
   10|       |//! kernel logic, but all of it is kernel *build time*, kernel binary size, and
   11|       |//! kernel CVE surface — and a crash in any of it is a crash in the kernel.
   12|       |//!
   13|       |//! tinybus moves those integrations behind a wire. An integration becomes a
   14|       |//! **service**: its own process, its own Cargo graph, its own crash domain,
   15|       |//! announcing a **well-known name** on the bus. The kernel keeps a
   16|       |//! [`Proxy`] and a `serde` derive, and nothing else.
   17|       |//!
   18|       |//! # The model, in one paragraph
   19|       |//!
   20|       |//! Same shape as D-Bus, because the shape is right and everyone already knows
   21|       |//! it. A [`Connection`] is one peer's link to the
   22|       |//! [`Broker`](broker::Broker). Every peer gets a unique name (`:1.7`) at
   23|       |//! connect time and may request well-known names
   24|       |//! (`ai.tinyhumans.openhuman.Voice`). A peer exports **objects** at
   25|       |//! [`ObjectPath`]s; each object implements one or more
   26|       |//! [`Interface`]s; an interface has **methods** you call
   27|       |//! and **signals** it emits. Calls are addressed
   28|       |//! `destination + path + interface + member`; signals are broadcast and
   29|       |//! delivered to whoever added a matching [`MatchRule`].
   30|       |//!
   31|       |//! # The shape of the crate
   32|       |//!
   33|       |//! Ports & adapters, following `tinysweeper`. [`ports`] holds one trait per
   34|       |//! file, every port has an always-compiled offline implementation, and only the
   35|       |//! socket-backed adapter sits behind a Cargo feature. That is what makes the
   36|       |//! test suite hermetic: [`transport::memory`] runs a broker, three services and
   37|       |//! a client inside one process with no file descriptors, so `cargo test` can
   38|       |//! assert on exact message ordering.
   39|       |//!
   40|       |//! ```no_run
   41|       |//! # async fn example() -> tinybus::Result<()> {
   42|       |//! use tinybus::{Connection, broker::Broker, transport::memory::MemoryBus};
   43|       |//!
   44|       |//! // A broker, a service and a client — all in one process, no sockets.
   45|       |//! let bus = MemoryBus::new();
   46|       |//! Broker::new().spawn(bus.clone());
   47|       |//!
   48|       |//! let client = Connection::connect(bus.connect().await?).await?;
   49|       |//! let voice = client.proxy(
   50|       |//!     "ai.tinyhumans.openhuman.Voice",
   51|       |//!     "/ai/tinyhumans/openhuman/Voice",
   52|       |//!     "ai.tinyhumans.openhuman.Voice",
   53|       |//! )?;
   54|       |//! let transcript: String = voice.call("Transcribe", ("/tmp/clip.wav",)).await?;
   55|       |//! # let _ = transcript;
   56|       |//! # Ok(())
   57|       |//! # }
   58|       |//! ```
   59|       |//!
   60|       |//! Modules land milestone by milestone; see `ROADMAP.md`.
   61|       |
   62|       |// `#[tinybus::interface]` expands to `::tinybus::…` paths, which do not resolve
   63|       |// inside this crate. The alias lets the macro be exercised by this crate's own
   64|       |// tests rather than only by a downstream one — the expansion is the part most
   65|       |// likely to break silently, so it has to be covered here.
   66|       |extern crate self as tinybus;
   67|       |

   69|       |pub mod connection;
   70|       |pub mod error;
   71|       |pub mod events;
   72|       |pub mod global;
   73|       |pub mod message;
   74|       |pub mod name;

   76|       |pub mod ports;
   77|       |pub mod proxy;
   78|       |pub mod router;
   79|       |pub mod service;

   81|       |pub mod version;

   86|       |

   88|       |pub use crate::error::{Error, Result};
   89|       |pub use crate::events::{
   90|       |    Event, EventBus, EventBusConfig, EventHandler, EventReceiver, SubscriptionHandle, TryRecvError,
   91|       |};

   95|       |pub use crate::native::{NativeRegistry, NativeRequestError};

  102|       |};

/home/enamakel/work/tinybus/worktrees/zbus-coverage-90/crates/tinybus/src/message/codec.rs:
    1|       |//! Framing: a 4-byte big-endian length, then that many bytes of JSON.
    2|       |//!
    3|       |//! Newline-delimited JSON would have been shorter to write and is what most
    4|       |//! JSON-RPC-over-socket code does. It is the wrong choice here for one reason:
    5|       |//! a body can legitimately contain a newline (a transcript, a mail body, an
    6|       |//! error backtrace), and the encoder would then have to escape it, meaning the
    7|       |//! frame boundary depends on the *content* of the payload. A length prefix
    8|       |//! makes reading a frame a fixed-cost operation that cannot be confused by
    9|       |//! anything a caller puts in the body, and it lets the reader reject an
   10|       |//! oversized frame before allocating for it.
   11|       |
   12|       |use serde::Serialize;
   13|       |use serde::de::DeserializeOwned;
   14|       |
   15|       |use crate::error::{Error, Result};
   16|       |
   17|       |/// The largest frame the reader will allocate for.
   18|       |///
   19|       |/// A hard cap, not a tunable. The frame length arrives from the wire *before*
   20|       |/// the bytes do, so without this the first four bytes of a hostile or corrupt
   21|       |/// stream are a 4 GiB allocation. 16 MiB is far above any legitimate control
   22|       |/// message; bulk payloads are supposed to travel as paths, not as bodies.
   23|       |pub const MAX_FRAME_LEN: usize = 16 * 1024 * 1024;
   24|       |
   25|       |/// The length prefix's width, in bytes.
   26|       |pub const LENGTH_PREFIX_LEN: usize = 4;
   27|       |
   28|       |/// Encode `value` into a length-prefixed frame.
   29|      3|pub fn encode<T: Serialize>(value: &T) -> Result<Vec<u8>> {
   30|      3|    let payload = serde_json::to_vec(value)?;
                                                         ^0
   31|      3|    if payload.len() > MAX_FRAME_LEN {
   32|      1|        return Err(Error::protocol(format!(
   33|      1|            "frame of {} bytes exceeds the {MAX_FRAME_LEN}-byte cap",
   34|      1|            payload.len()
   35|      1|        )));
   36|      2|    }
   37|      2|    let mut frame = Vec::with_capacity(LENGTH_PREFIX_LEN + payload.len());
   38|      2|    frame.extend_from_slice(&(payload.len() as u32).to_be_bytes());
   39|      2|    frame.extend_from_slice(&payload);
   40|      2|    Ok(frame)
   41|      3|}
   42|       |
   43|       |/// Read the payload length out of a length prefix, rejecting oversized frames.
   44|      3|pub fn decode_length(prefix: [u8; LENGTH_PREFIX_LEN]) -> Result<usize> {
   45|      3|    let len = u32::from_be_bytes(prefix) as usize;
   46|      3|    if len > MAX_FRAME_LEN {
   47|      1|        return Err(Error::protocol(format!(
   48|      1|            "peer announced a {len}-byte frame, over the {MAX_FRAME_LEN}-byte cap"
   49|      1|        )));
   50|      2|    }
   51|      2|    Ok(len)
   52|      3|}
   53|       |
   54|       |/// Decode a payload that has already been read in full.
   55|      2|pub fn decode<T: DeserializeOwned>(payload: &[u8]) -> Result<T> {
   56|      2|    Ok(serde_json::from_slice(payload)?)
                                                    ^0
   57|      2|}
   58|       |

/home/enamakel/work/tinybus/worktrees/zbus-coverage-90/crates/tinybus/src/message/mod.rs:
    1|       |//! The wire format: one [`Message`] type, four kinds, JSON bodies.
    2|       |//!
    3|       |//! # Why JSON and not a binary encoding
    4|       |//!
    5|       |//! D-Bus has its own marshalling; we deliberately do not. The integrations on
    6|       |//! this bus are things like "transcribe a file" and "sign a transaction" — the
    7|       |//! call rate is human-scale, and the body is dwarfed by the work it triggers.
    8|       |//! What we get in exchange is that `tinybus monitor` is readable, a service can
    9|       |//! be written in any language in an afternoon, and `serde` derives on the
   10|       |//! kernel side are the entire client binding. The one place this would be the
   11|       |//! wrong trade is bulk binary payloads (audio, PDFs), and that is exactly what
   12|       |//! `ROADMAP.md`'s file-descriptor passing milestone is for; until then large
   13|       |//! payloads travel as paths, not as base64.
   14|       |//!
   15|       |//! # Why the header is flat
   16|       |//!
   17|       |//! Routing reads `destination` and nothing else. Keeping it a flat struct of
   18|       |//! `Option`s rather than an enum per kind means the broker can route a message
   19|       |//! without knowing what kind it is, which is what lets an unknown future kind
   20|       |//! pass through an old broker instead of being dropped.
   21|       |
   22|       |pub mod codec;
   23|       |
   24|       |use serde::{Deserialize, Serialize};
   25|       |use serde_json::Value;
   26|       |
   27|       |use crate::error::{Error, Result};
   28|       |use crate::name::{BusName, InterfaceName, MemberName, ObjectPath};
   29|       |
   30|       |/// What a message is for.
   31|       |///
   32|       |/// Non-exhaustive on purpose: a broker that meets a kind it does not know still
   33|       |/// routes it, because routing only needs the destination.
   34|       |#[derive(Debug, Clone, Copy, PartialEq, Eq, Hash, Serialize, Deserialize)]
   35|       |#[serde(rename_all = "snake_case")]
   36|       |#[non_exhaustive]
   37|       |pub enum MessageKind {
   38|       |    /// A request for a method to run, expecting exactly one reply.
   39|       |    MethodCall,
   40|       |    /// The successful reply to a [`MessageKind::MethodCall`].
   41|       |    MethodReturn,
   42|       |    /// The failed reply to a [`MessageKind::MethodCall`].
   43|       |    Error,
   44|       |    /// A broadcast. Has no reply and no destination; delivery is decided by
   45|       |    /// each peer's [`crate::router::MatchRule`]s.
   46|       |    Signal,
   47|       |}
   48|       |
   49|       |/// Everything the router and the dispatcher need, and nothing else.
   50|       |#[derive(Debug, Clone, PartialEq, Serialize, Deserialize)]
   51|       |pub struct Header {
   52|       |    /// What this message is for.
   53|       |    pub kind: MessageKind,
   54|       |    /// Per-connection, monotonically increasing. Unique only within a sender.
   55|       |    pub serial: u64,
   56|       |    /// For replies: the `serial` of the call being answered.
   57|       |    #[serde(default, skip_serializing_if = "Option::is_none")]
   58|       |    pub reply_serial: Option<u64>,
   59|       |    /// Stamped by the broker, never trusted from the peer.
   60|       |    ///
   61|       |    /// A service authorising a call reads this field; if peers could set it,
   62|       |    /// every authorisation decision on the bus would be forgeable.
   63|       |    #[serde(default, skip_serializing_if = "Option::is_none")]
   64|       |    pub sender: Option<BusName>,
   65|       |    /// Who the message is for. `None` on signals, which are broadcast.
   66|       |    #[serde(default, skip_serializing_if = "Option::is_none")]
   67|       |    pub destination: Option<BusName>,
   68|       |    /// The object being addressed.
   69|       |    #[serde(default, skip_serializing_if = "Option::is_none")]
   70|       |    pub path: Option<ObjectPath>,
   71|       |    /// The interface the member belongs to.
   72|       |    #[serde(default, skip_serializing_if = "Option::is_none")]
   73|       |    pub interface: Option<InterfaceName>,
   74|       |    /// The method or signal name.
   75|       |    #[serde(default, skip_serializing_if = "Option::is_none")]
   76|       |    pub member: Option<MemberName>,
   77|       |    /// For [`MessageKind::Error`]: the stable dotted error name.
   78|       |    #[serde(default, skip_serializing_if = "Option::is_none")]
   79|       |    pub error_name: Option<String>,
   80|       |}
   81|       |
   82|       |/// A framed message: header plus a JSON body.
   83|       |///
   84|       |/// The body is positional — a JSON array of arguments for a call, a single
   85|       |/// value for a return. Positional rather than named because it makes the
   86|       |/// generated dispatch in `#[interface]` a plain tuple deserialize, and because
   87|       |/// a named body invites callers to depend on parameter *names*, which are an
   88|       |/// implementation detail of the service's Rust signature.
   89|       |#[derive(Debug, Clone, PartialEq, Serialize, Deserialize)]
   90|       |pub struct Message {
   91|       |    /// Routing and dispatch metadata.
   92|       |    pub header: Header,
   93|       |    /// The payload. `Value::Null` when there is none, and omitted from the
   94|       |    /// wire in that case — a bus carrying mostly argument-less signals should
   95|       |    /// not spend four bytes per message saying so.
   96|       |    #[serde(default, skip_serializing_if = "Value::is_null")]
   97|       |    pub body: Value,
   98|       |}

  219|       |    /// Check the invariants the router relies on before a message is accepted

  225|  3.39k|    pub fn validate(&self) -> Result<()> {

/home/enamakel/work/tinybus/worktrees/zbus-coverage-90/crates/tinybus/src/name.rs:
    1|       |//! The four validated newtypes every address on the bus is made of.
    2|       |//!
    3|       |//! Addresses are parsed once, at construction, and are infallible thereafter.
    4|       |//! The router therefore never has to ask whether a destination is well-formed —
    5|       |//! a [`BusName`] that exists is a [`BusName`] that is valid. This is the same
    6|       |//! reason D-Bus validates names at the bus rather than at the endpoint: a
    7|       |//! malformed address is a routing decision you cannot make, and discovering
    8|       |//! that halfway through a dispatch table is how messages get delivered to the
    9|       |//! wrong peer.
   10|       |//!
   11|       |//! The grammars are deliberately narrower than D-Bus's. Interface and bus names
   12|       |//! are ASCII-only and disallow leading digits per element, so a name is always
   13|       |//! a legal identifier in Rust, in a filename, and in a log line that someone
   14|       |//! will eventually grep.
   15|       |
   16|       |use std::fmt;
   17|       |use std::str::FromStr;
   18|       |
   19|       |use serde::{Deserialize, Serialize};
   20|       |
   21|       |use crate::error::{Error, Result};
   22|       |

   24|     13|    Error::InvalidName {
   25|     13|        kind,
   26|     13|        input: input.to_string(),
   27|     13|        reason: reason.to_string(),
   28|     13|    }
   29|     13|}

   84|       |/// A peer's address on the bus.
   85|       |///
   86|       |/// Two flavours share one type, because every routing decision treats them
   87|       |/// identically:
   88|       |///
   89|       |/// - **Unique** — `:1.7`, assigned by the broker at connect time, never reused
   90|       |///   for the lifetime of the broker, and released when the peer disconnects.
   91|       |/// - **Well-known** — `ai.tinyhumans.openhuman.Voice`, requested by a service
   92|       |///   and owned by at most one peer at a time. This is what the kernel targets;
   93|       |///   it does not care which process, or which restart of it, answers.

/home/enamakel/work/tinybus/worktrees/zbus-coverage-90/crates/tinybus/src/native.rs:
    1|       |//! In-process typed request/response, with no serialization at all.
    2|       |//!
    3|       |//! This is OpenHuman's `core::event_bus::native_request`, ported unchanged in
    4|       |//! behaviour. It lives in the bus crate for one reason: without it, a host that
    5|       |//! moved to tinybus would still need its own second bus for the calls that
    6|       |//! cannot be serialized, and "which bus does this go on" would be a question
    7|       |//! every domain had to answer twice.
    8|       |//!
    9|       |//! # Why this exists next to a perfectly good wire protocol
   10|       |//!
   11|       |//! The rest of tinybus turns a call into JSON and puts it on a socket. That is
   12|       |//! the right trade for an integration in another process, and the wrong one for
   13|       |//! two modules in the same address space passing things that have no
   14|       |//! serialized form at all:
   15|       |//!
   16|       |//! ```text
   17|       |//! AgentTurnRequest {
   18|       |//!     parent_tools: Arc<Vec<Box<dyn Tool>>>,   // trait objects
   19|       |//!     on_progress:  Option<Sender<Progress>>,  // a live channel
   20|       |//!     run_queue:    Option<Arc<RunQueue>>,     // shared mutable state
   21|       |//! }
   22|       |//! ```
   23|       |//!
   24|       |//! There is no encoding of `Arc<dyn Tool>` that survives a process boundary,
   25|       |//! and inventing one would mean the receiver getting a *copy* of something
   26|       |//! whose whole purpose is being shared. So this surface stays in-process,
   27|       |//! permanently and by design — it is not a milestone that has not landed yet.
   28|       |//!
   29|       |//! The rule for choosing, stated once:
   30|       |//!
   31|       |//! | Need | Surface |
   32|       |//! | --- | --- |
   33|       |//! | notify anyone who cares | [`crate::events`] |
   34|       |//! | call another *process* | [`crate::Proxy`] |
   35|       |//! | call another module with a non-serializable payload | this |
   36|       |//!
   37|       |//! # Sync vs async
   38|       |//!
   39|       |//! Registration is **sync** — it is a `HashMap::insert` under a std lock, so
   40|       |//! startup code in a `Once::call_once` or a plain `fn main` can register
   41|       |//! without a runtime. Dispatch is **async**, and takes care to clone the
   42|       |//! handler's `Arc` and drop the lock *before* awaiting, so a slow handler never
   43|       |//! blocks an unrelated dispatch.
   44|       |
   45|       |use std::any::{Any, TypeId};
   46|       |use std::collections::HashMap;
   47|       |use std::future::Future;
   48|       |use std::pin::Pin;
   49|       |use std::sync::{Arc, RwLock};
   50|       |
   51|       |/// Errors raised by the native request surface.
   52|       |///
   53|       |/// Separate from [`crate::Error`] on purpose: nothing here can cross the wire,
   54|       |/// so none of it has — or should have — a dotted wire name.
   55|       |#[derive(Debug, Clone, PartialEq, Eq)]
   56|       |pub enum NativeRequestError {
   57|       |    /// No handler is registered for the method.
   58|       |    UnregisteredHandler {
   59|       |        /// The method that was called.
   60|       |        method: String,
   61|       |    },
   62|       |    /// Caller and handler disagree on the request or response type.
   63|       |    ///
   64|       |    /// This is the failure the `TypeId` check exists to turn into an error
   65|       |    /// rather than a transmute: two modules compiled against different versions
   66|       |    /// of a shared request struct would otherwise reinterpret each other's
   67|       |    /// memory.
   68|       |    TypeMismatch {
   69|       |        /// The method that was called.
   70|       |        method: String,
   71|       |        /// The type the handler registered.
   72|       |        expected: &'static str,
   73|       |        /// The type the caller supplied.
   74|       |        actual: &'static str,
   75|       |    },
   76|       |    /// The handler ran and returned an error.
   77|       |    HandlerFailed {
   78|       |        /// The method that was called.
   79|       |        method: String,
   80|       |        /// What the handler said.
   81|       |        message: String,
   82|       |    },
   83|       |}
   84|       |
   85|       |impl std::fmt::Display for NativeRequestError {
   86|      0|    fn fmt(&self, f: &mut std::fmt::Formatter<'_>) -> std::fmt::Result {
   87|      0|        match self {
   88|      0|            Self::UnregisteredHandler { method } => {
   89|      0|                write!(f, "no native handler registered for method '{method}'")
   90|       |            }
   91|       |            Self::TypeMismatch {
   92|      0|                method,
   93|      0|                expected,
   94|      0|                actual,
   95|      0|            } => write!(
   96|      0|                f,
   97|       |                "native handler type mismatch for '{method}': expected {expected}, got {actual}"
   98|       |            ),
   99|      0|            Self::HandlerFailed { method, message } => {
  100|      0|                write!(f, "native handler '{method}' failed: {message}")
  101|       |            }
  102|       |        }
  103|      0|    }
  104|       |}
  105|       |

  109|       |type HandlerFuture = Pin<Box<dyn Future<Output = Result<BoxedAny, String>> + Send>>;
  110|       |type BoxedHandler = Arc<dyn Fn(BoxedAny) -> HandlerFuture + Send + Sync>;
  111|       |

  120|       |/// A registry of in-process, Rust-typed request handlers, keyed by method name.
  121|       |#[derive(Clone, Default)]
  122|       |pub struct NativeRegistry {
  123|       |    handlers: Arc<RwLock<HashMap<String, HandlerEntry>>>,
  124|       |}
  125|       |
  126|       |impl std::fmt::Debug for NativeRegistry {
  127|      0|    fn fmt(&self, f: &mut std::fmt::Formatter<'_>) -> std::fmt::Result {
  128|       |        // A non-blocking read: a `Debug` that can deadlock is a `Debug` that
  129|       |        // makes a hung process impossible to inspect.
  130|      0|        match self.handlers.try_read() {
  131|      0|            Ok(guard) => f
  132|      0|                .debug_struct("NativeRegistry")
  133|      0|                .field("methods", &guard.keys().collect::<Vec<_>>())
  134|      0|                .finish(),
  135|      0|            Err(_) => f
  136|      0|                .debug_struct("NativeRegistry")
  137|      0|                .field("methods", &"<locked>")
  138|      0|                .finish(),
  139|       |        }
  140|      0|    }

  143|       |/// Recover from lock poisoning by taking the inner guard.
  144|       |///
  145|       |/// The registry holds a plain `HashMap`; a panic elsewhere while holding the
  146|       |/// lock cannot have left it in a state that matters. Propagating the poison
  147|       |/// would turn one unrelated panic into every subsequent dispatch failing.

  149|     30|    result.unwrap_or_else(|e| e.into_inner())
                                            ^1^1

  198|       |    /// Dispatch a typed request.
  199|     10|    pub async fn request<Req, Resp>(
  200|     10|        &self,
  201|     10|        method: &str,
  202|     10|        req: Req,
  203|     10|    ) -> Result<Resp, NativeRequestError>
  204|     10|    where
  205|     10|        Req: Send + 'static,
  206|     10|        Resp: Send + 'static,
  207|     10|    {

  209|       |        // std lock across an await would both risk deadlock and serialise every
  210|       |        // dispatch behind the slowest handler.
  211|      9|        let (handler, req_type, resp_type, req_name, resp_name) = {
  212|     10|            let guard = unpoison(self.handlers.read());
  213|      9|            let entry =
  214|     10|                guard
  215|     10|                    .get(method)
  216|     10|                    .ok_or_else(|| NativeRequestError::UnregisteredHandler {
  217|      1|                        method: method.to_string(),
  218|      1|                    })?;
  219|      9|            (
  220|      9|                Arc::clone(&entry.handler),
  221|      9|                entry.req_type,
  222|      9|                entry.resp_type,
  223|      9|                entry.req_name,
  224|      9|                entry.resp_name,
  225|      9|            )
  226|       |        };
  227|       |
  228|      9|        if TypeId::of::<Req>() != req_type {
  229|      1|            return Err(NativeRequestError::TypeMismatch {
  230|      1|                method: method.to_string(),
  231|      1|                expected: req_name,
  232|      1|                actual: std::any::type_name::<Req>(),
  233|      1|            });
  234|      8|        }
  235|      8|        if TypeId::of::<Resp>() != resp_type {
  236|      1|            return Err(NativeRequestError::TypeMismatch {
  237|      1|                method: method.to_string(),
  238|      1|                expected: resp_name,
  239|      1|                actual: std::any::type_name::<Resp>(),
  240|      1|            });
  241|      7|        }
  242|       |
  243|      7|        match handler(Box::new(req)).await {
  244|      6|            Ok(boxed) => Ok(*boxed.downcast::<Resp>().expect(
  245|      6|                "native: handler returned the wrong response type despite the TypeId check",
  246|      6|            )),
  247|      1|            Err(message) => Err(NativeRequestError::HandlerFailed {
  248|      1|                method: method.to_string(),
  249|      1|                message,
  250|      1|            }),
  251|       |        }

    2|       |//!
    3|       |//! Separate from [`crate::ports::Transport`] because the broker is the only
    4|       |//! thing that accepts, while everything on the bus transports. Keeping them
    5|       |//! apart means a service links the accept path out entirely.

   16|       |    ///
   17|       |    /// An implementation must treat a *per-connection* failure (a peer that
   18|       |    /// dies mid-handshake) as something to skip, not as a reason to return an

   21|       |

   24|      0|        "listener".to_string()
   25|      0|    }

/home/enamakel/work/tinybus/worktrees/zbus-coverage-90/crates/tinybus/src/ports/transport.rs:
    1|       |//! The transport port: a bidirectional, ordered, framed message stream.
    2|       |//!
    3|       |//! # The contract
    4|       |//!
    5|       |//! - **Ordered.** Messages arrive in send order. The router assumes it; signal
    6|       |//!   ordering relative to the reply that caused them is observable behaviour.
    7|       |//! - **Framed.** `recv` yields whole [`Message`]s or nothing. Partial frames
    8|       |//!   are the implementation's problem, never the caller's.
    9|       |//! - **`recv` is called from exactly one task.** Both the connection and the
   10|       |//!   broker's peer loop own a single reader task per transport, so an
   11|       |//!   implementation may hold a lock across the await in `recv` without
   12|       |//!   deadlocking. `send` has no such restriction and must be callable
   13|       |//!   concurrently — every proxy on a connection shares one transport.
   14|       |//! - **`Ok(None)` from `recv` means clean shutdown**, and is not an error. A
   15|       |//!   service exiting normally must not log a stack of transport errors on the
   16|       |//!   kernel side.
   17|       |
   18|       |use async_trait::async_trait;
   19|       |
   20|       |use crate::error::Result;
   21|       |use crate::message::Message;
   22|       |
   23|       |/// One peer's bidirectional link, framed at the message level.
   24|       |#[async_trait]
   25|       |pub trait Transport: Send + Sync + 'static {
   26|       |    /// Write one message. Must be safe to call from many tasks at once.
   27|       |    async fn send(&self, message: Message) -> Result<()>;
   28|       |
   29|       |    /// Read the next message, or `Ok(None)` once the peer has hung up cleanly.
   30|       |    ///
   31|       |    /// Called from a single task per transport; see the module docs.
   32|       |    async fn recv(&self) -> Result<Option<Message>>;
   33|       |
   34|       |    /// Close the link. Idempotent: shutdown races are normal, and a second
   35|       |    /// close must not be an error.
   36|       |    async fn close(&self) -> Result<()>;
   37|       |
   38|       |    /// A short label for logs and `tinybus monitor`, e.g. `memory` or
   39|       |    /// `unix:/run/user/1000/tinybus`. Never a credential.
   40|      0|    fn describe(&self) -> String {
   41|      0|        "transport".to_string()
   42|      0|    }
   43|       |}

    1|       |//! The client side: a typed handle to one interface on one remote object.
    2|       |//!
    3|       |//! This is the entire surface the OpenHuman kernel is meant to depend on. A
    4|       |//! kernel that used to link `whisper-rs`, `cpal` and a model runtime to
    5|       |//! transcribe audio now does this:
    6|       |//!
    7|       |//! ```no_run

    9|       |//! let voice = connection.proxy(

   11|       |//!     "/ai/tinyhumans/openhuman/Voice",
   12|       |//!     "ai.tinyhumans.openhuman.Voice",

   21|       |//! used to: `R` is checked against what actually came back, so a service that

/home/enamakel/work/tinybus/worktrees/zbus-coverage-90/crates/tinybus/src/router.rs:
    1|       |//! The routing table and the match rules that drive signal delivery.
    2|       |//!
    3|       |//! The router is deliberately dumb and deliberately synchronous. It answers two
    4|       |//! questions — "which peer owns this name" and "which peers want this signal" —
    5|       |//! and it answers them without awaiting anything, so the lock it lives under is
    6|       |//! never held across a suspend point. Callers take the answer (a set of cloned
    7|       |//! channel senders), drop the lock, and only then do the sending. Getting this
    8|       |//! wrong is the classic broker deadlock: peer A's slow queue holds the routing
    9|       |//! lock while peer B is trying to disconnect.
   10|       |//!
   11|       |//! # Match rules
   12|       |//!
   13|       |//! A signal has no destination, so a peer states what it wants with a rule:
   14|       |//!
   15|       |//! ```text
   16|       |//! type=signal,interface=ai.tinyhumans.openhuman.Mail,member=Received
   17|       |//! type=signal,path_namespace=/ai/tinyhumans/openhuman/Mail
   18|       |//! sender=ai.tinyhumans.tinybus.Bus,member=NameOwnerChanged
   19|       |//! ```
   20|       |//!
   21|       |//! Unset fields match anything; set fields must all match. Filtering happens at
   22|       |//! the *broker*, not at the client, because the whole point of the exercise is
   23|       |//! that the kernel does not pay for integrations it is not using — waking it up
   24|       |//! to discard a signal it never asked for is exactly that cost in miniature.
   25|       |
   26|       |use std::collections::HashMap;
   27|       |
   28|       |use tokio::sync::mpsc;
   29|       |
   30|       |use crate::error::{Error, Result};
   31|       |use crate::message::{Message, MessageKind};
   32|       |use crate::name::{BusName, InterfaceName, MemberName, ObjectPath};
   33|       |use crate::version::{PeerManifest, PeerRecord};
   34|       |
   35|       |/// A subscription filter. Every set field must match; unset fields match all.
   36|       |#[derive(Debug, Clone, Default, PartialEq, Eq)]
   37|       |pub struct MatchRule {
   38|       |    /// Restrict to one message kind. Almost always [`MessageKind::Signal`].
   39|       |    pub kind: Option<MessageKind>,
   40|       |    /// Restrict to one sender, by unique or well-known name.
   41|       |    pub sender: Option<BusName>,
   42|       |    /// Restrict to one interface.
   43|       |    pub interface: Option<InterfaceName>,
   44|       |    /// Restrict to one member.
   45|       |    pub member: Option<MemberName>,
   46|       |    /// Restrict to one exact object path.
   47|       |    pub path: Option<ObjectPath>,
   48|       |    /// Restrict to a path and everything beneath it.
   49|       |    ///
   50|       |    /// Separate from `path` rather than a flag on it, because "this mailbox"
   51|       |    /// and "every mailbox" are different subscriptions and conflating them is
   52|       |    /// how a client ends up quietly receiving another account's traffic.
   53|       |    pub path_namespace: Option<ObjectPath>,
   54|       |}
   55|       |
   56|       |impl MatchRule {
   57|       |    /// An empty rule, which matches every signal. Build it up with the setters.
   58|     45|    pub fn new() -> Self {
   59|     45|        Self::default()
   60|     45|    }
   61|       |
   62|       |    /// Restrict to signals.
   63|     20|    pub fn signals(mut self) -> Self {
   64|     20|        self.kind = Some(MessageKind::Signal);
   65|     20|        self
   66|     20|    }
   67|       |
   68|       |    /// Restrict to one interface.
   69|     15|    pub fn interface(mut self, interface: InterfaceName) -> Self {
   70|     15|        self.interface = Some(interface);
   71|     15|        self
   72|     15|    }
   73|       |
   74|       |    /// Restrict to one member.
   75|      1|    pub fn member(mut self, member: MemberName) -> Self {
   76|      1|        self.member = Some(member);
   77|      1|        self
   78|      1|    }
   79|       |
   80|       |    /// Restrict to one sender.
   81|      0|    pub fn sender(mut self, sender: BusName) -> Self {
   82|      0|        self.sender = Some(sender);
   83|      0|        self
   84|      0|    }
   85|       |
   86|       |    /// Restrict to a path and its subtree.
   87|     13|    pub fn path_namespace(mut self, path: ObjectPath) -> Self {
   88|     13|        self.path_namespace = Some(path);
   89|     13|        self
   90|     13|    }
   91|       |
   92|       |    /// Whether `message` satisfies every set field.
