  129|       |    /// Parse the comma-separated `key=value` wire form.
  130|       |    ///
  131|       |    /// Values are unquoted and may not contain a comma. That is a real
  132|       |    /// restriction, and it is fine: every field is a name, and no name grammar
  133|       |    /// in [`crate::name`] admits a comma.
  134|     24|    pub fn parse(input: &str) -> Result<Self> {
  135|     24|        let mut rule = Self::new();
  136|     60|        for clause in input.split(',').filter(|c| !c.trim().is_empty()) {
                                    ^24   ^24        ^24
  137|     60|            let (key, value) = clause
                               ^59  ^59
  138|     60|                .split_once('=')
  139|     60|                .ok_or_else(|| Error::protocol(format!("match clause `{clause}` has no `=`")))?;
                                             ^1              ^1                                             ^1
  140|     59|            let value = value.trim();
  141|     59|            match key.trim() {
  142|     59|                "type" => {
  143|     21|                    rule.kind = Some(match value {
  144|     21|                        "signal" => MessageKind::Signal,
                                                  ^20
  145|      1|                        "method_call" => MessageKind::MethodCall,
                                                       ^0
  146|      1|                        "method_return" => MessageKind::MethodReturn,
                                                         ^0
  147|      1|                        "error" => MessageKind::Error,
                                                 ^0
  148|      1|                        other => {
  149|      1|                            return Err(Error::protocol(format!("unknown message type `{other}`")));
  150|       |                        }
  151|       |                    });
  152|       |                }
  153|     38|                "sender" => rule.sender = Some(BusName::new(value)?),
                                          ^0                 ^0           ^0    ^0
  154|     38|                "interface" => rule.interface = Some(InterfaceName::new(value)?),
                                             ^17                   ^17                ^17   ^0
  155|     21|                "member" => rule.member = Some(MemberName::new(value)?),
                                          ^4                 ^4              ^4    ^0
  156|     17|                "path" => rule.path = Some(ObjectPath::new(value)?),
                                        ^0               ^0              ^0    ^0
  157|     17|                "path_namespace" => rule.path_namespace = Some(ObjectPath::new(value)?),
                                                  ^16                        ^16             ^16   ^0
  158|      1|                other => {
  159|      1|                    return Err(Error::protocol(format!("unknown match key `{other}`")));
  160|       |                }
  161|       |            }
  162|       |        }
  163|     21|        Ok(rule)
  164|     24|    }

  166|       |
  167|       |/// One attached peer, from the broker's point of view.
  168|       |struct Peer {
  169|       |    unique: BusName,
  170|       |    /// The writer task's inbox. Cloned out of the table and sent to *after*
  171|       |    /// the routing lock is released.
  172|       |    outbox: mpsc::Sender<Message>,
  173|       |    matches: Vec<MatchRule>,
  174|       |    /// What this peer says it speaks and accepts. `None` until it announces —
  175|       |    /// and a peer that never announces stays routable, so manifests can be
  176|       |    /// adopted one service at a time rather than as a flag day.
  177|       |    manifest: Option<PeerManifest>,
  178|       |}
  179|       |
  180|       |/// Who is attached, what they are called, and what they want to hear.
  181|       |///
  182|       |/// Not `Sync` by itself — the broker wraps it in a plain `std::sync::Mutex`,
  183|       |/// which is only sound because no method here awaits.
  184|       |#[derive(Default)]
  185|       |pub(crate) struct Router {
  186|       |    peers: HashMap<u64, Peer>,
  187|       |    names: HashMap<BusName, u64>,
  188|       |    next_id: u64,
  189|       |}
  190|       |

  194|       |    pub name: BusName,
  195|       |    pub old_owner: Option<BusName>,

  197|       |}

  201|     79|    pub fn attach(&mut self, outbox: mpsc::Sender<Message>) -> (u64, BusName) {

  203|       |        // dead `:1.4` can never be delivered to its replacement.

  249|       |    /// Claim a well-known name for `id`.
  250|       |    ///
  251|       |    /// First writer wins and there is no queue: a second claimant is told the
  252|       |    /// name is taken rather than being parked. Queued ownership is a D-Bus
  253|       |    /// feature we are consciously not copying — two live processes both able to
  254|       |    /// answer as the wallet is a worse outcome than a clear startup failure.
  255|     32|    pub fn request_name(&mut self, id: u64, name: BusName) -> Result<NameChange> {

  257|      1|            return Err(Error::protocol("a unique name cannot be requested"));
  258|     31|        }

  260|      2|            return Err(Error::protocol("the bus's own name is reserved"));
  261|     29|        }

  263|      3|            Some(owner) if *owner == id => Ok(NameChange {
                               ^1                   ^1
  264|      1|                name: name.clone(),
  265|      1|                old_owner: Some(self.unique_of(id)?),
                                                                ^0
  266|      1|                new_owner: Some(self.unique_of(id)?),
                                                                ^0
  267|       |            }),
  268|      2|            Some(owner) => Err(Error::NameTaken {
  269|      2|                name,
  270|      2|                owner: self.unique_of(*owner)?,
                                                           ^0
  271|       |            }),

  273|     26|                self.names.insert(name.clone(), id);
  274|       |                Ok(NameChange {
  275|     26|                    name,
  276|     26|                    old_owner: None,
  277|     26|                    new_owner: Some(self.unique_of(id)?),

  278|       |                })
  279|       |            }

    1|       |//! End-to-end coverage for what `#[tinybus::interface]` generates.
    2|       |//!
    3|       |//! The expansion is the part of tinybus most likely to break silently: a
    4|       |//! mistake in argument decoding or member naming produces code that compiles
    5|       |//! and then fails at the first call, in a *different* process, as an opaque
    6|       |//! error. So these tests exercise it the way a real integration does — over a

/home/enamakel/work/tinybus/worktrees/zbus-coverage-90/crates/tinybus/src/service/mod.rs:
    1|       |//! The service side: what an integration exports, and how a call finds it.
    2|       |//!
    3|       |//! An integration implements [`Interface`] — usually by writing a normal `impl`
    4|       |//! block and putting `#[tinybus::interface]` on it — and registers instances at
    5|       |//! [`ObjectPath`](crate::name::ObjectPath)s on its connection. Dispatch is then
    6|       |//! `path → interface → member`, in that order, with a distinct error at each
    7|       |//! step so a caller can tell "that integration isn't running" from "that
    8|       |//! integration is running an older contract".
    9|       |//!
   10|       |//! # Why the trait takes and returns [`Value`]
   11|       |//!
   12|       |//! Typed dispatch is what the macro *generates*; the trait underneath has to be
   13|       |//! object-safe, because one connection holds a heterogeneous list of interfaces
   14|       |//! behind `dyn`. The macro turns a typed Rust signature into an argument
   15|       |//! deserialize, a call, and a return serialize — so the handwritten code is
   16|       |//! typed and only the seam is dynamic.
   17|       |
   18|       |pub mod tree;
   19|       |
   20|       |#[cfg(test)]
   21|       |mod macro_test;
   22|       |
   23|       |use async_trait::async_trait;
   24|       |use serde_json::Value;
   25|       |
   26|       |use crate::error::Result;
   27|       |use crate::name::{InterfaceName, MemberName};
   28|       |
   29|       |pub use crate::service::tree::ObjectTree;
   30|       |
   31|       |/// One contract a service implements.
   32|       |///
   33|       |/// Implement this by hand only for dynamic interfaces — a bridge that proxies
   34|       |/// an interface it does not know at compile time. Everything else should use
   35|       |/// `#[tinybus::interface]`.
   36|       |#[async_trait]
   37|       |pub trait Interface: Send + Sync + 'static {
   38|       |    /// The interface's name, e.g. `ai.tinyhumans.openhuman.Voice`.
   39|       |    fn name(&self) -> InterfaceName;
   40|       |
   41|       |    /// Every member this interface dispatches, for introspection.
   42|       |    ///
   43|       |    /// `tinybus call` uses it to fail fast with the available members listed,
   44|       |    /// which is the difference between a typo taking ten seconds and ten
   45|       |    /// minutes.
   46|       |    fn members(&self) -> Vec<MemberName>;
   47|       |
   48|       |    /// Run `member` against `args`, a positional JSON array.
   49|       |    ///
   50|       |    /// Returning `Err` is normal and expected: it becomes an error reply, and
   51|       |    /// the caller sees [`crate::Error::MethodFailed`] carrying
   52|       |    /// [`crate::Error::wire_name`]. A panic, by contrast, takes the whole
   53|       |    /// service down — [`crate::connection::Connection`] does not catch unwinds,
   54|       |    /// because a service that has panicked has an unknown internal state and
   55|       |    /// answering the next call from it is worse than being restarted.
   56|       |    async fn call(&self, member: &MemberName, args: Value) -> Result<Value>;
   57|       |}
   58|       |
   59|       |#[async_trait]
   60|       |impl<T: Interface + ?Sized> Interface for std::sync::Arc<T> {
   61|     76|    fn name(&self) -> InterfaceName {
   62|     76|        (**self).name()
   63|     76|    }
   64|       |
   65|     29|    fn members(&self) -> Vec<MemberName> {
   66|     29|        (**self).members()
   67|     29|    }
   68|       |
   69|     24|    async fn call(&self, member: &MemberName, args: Value) -> Result<Value> {
   70|       |        (**self).call(member, args).await
   71|     24|    }
   72|       |}

/home/enamakel/work/tinybus/worktrees/zbus-coverage-90/crates/tinybus/src/service/tree.rs:
    1|       |//! The object tree: what this connection exports, and where.
    2|       |//!
    3|       |//! A flat map from [`ObjectPath`] to a list of interfaces. Flat rather than an
    4|       |//! actual tree because nothing needs the hierarchy at dispatch time — the only
    5|       |//! consumer of path structure is `path_namespace` matching, which is a string
    6|       |//! comparison on the sender's side. A real tree would buy nothing and would
    7|       |//! make "list every object" — the operation introspection actually performs —
    8|       |//! a traversal instead of an iteration.
    9|       |
   10|       |use std::collections::HashMap;
   11|       |use std::sync::Arc;
   12|       |
   13|       |use serde_json::Value;
   14|       |
   15|       |use crate::error::{Error, Result};
   16|       |use crate::name::{InterfaceName, MemberName, ObjectPath};
   17|       |use crate::service::Interface;
   18|       |
   19|       |/// Everything one connection exports.
   20|       |#[derive(Default)]
   21|       |pub struct ObjectTree {
   22|       |    objects: HashMap<ObjectPath, Vec<Arc<dyn Interface>>>,
   23|       |}
   24|       |
   25|       |impl ObjectTree {
   26|       |    /// An empty tree.
   27|     84|    pub fn new() -> Self {
   28|     84|        Self::default()
   29|     84|    }
   30|       |
   31|       |    /// Export `interface` at `path`.
   32|       |    ///
   33|       |    /// Registering a second interface with a name already present at that path
   34|       |    /// **replaces** it. Replace rather than reject because hot-reloading an
   35|       |    /// implementation is a legitimate thing for a long-lived service to do, and
   36|       |    /// two implementations of one contract at one address would make dispatch
   37|       |    /// order-dependent.
   38|     35|    pub fn insert(&mut self, path: ObjectPath, interface: Arc<dyn Interface>) {
   39|     35|        let name = interface.name();
   40|     35|        let entry = self.objects.entry(path).or_default();
   41|     35|        entry.retain(|existing| existing.name() != name);
                                              ^2                 ^2
   42|     35|        entry.push(interface);
   43|     35|    }
   44|       |
   45|       |    /// Stop exporting everything at `path`. Returns whether anything went away.
   46|      2|    pub fn remove(&mut self, path: &ObjectPath) -> bool {
   47|      2|        self.objects.remove(path).is_some()
   48|      2|    }
   49|       |
   50|       |    /// Every exported path, sorted, for introspection.
   51|      1|    pub fn paths(&self) -> Vec<ObjectPath> {
   52|      1|        let mut paths: Vec<ObjectPath> = self.objects.keys().cloned().collect();
   53|      1|        paths.sort();
   54|      1|        paths
   55|      1|    }
   56|       |
   57|       |    /// The interfaces exported at `path`.
   58|      2|    pub fn interfaces_at(&self, path: &ObjectPath) -> Vec<InterfaceName> {
   59|      2|        self.objects
   60|      2|            .get(path)
   61|      3|            .map(|list| list.iter().map(|i| i.name()).collect())
                           ^2         ^2          ^2                ^2
   62|      2|            .unwrap_or_default()
   63|      2|    }
   64|       |
   65|       |    /// Look up one interface, distinguishing "no such object" from
   66|       |    /// "object exists, wrong contract".
   67|     23|    pub fn lookup(
   68|     23|        &self,
   69|     23|        path: &ObjectPath,
   70|     23|        interface: &InterfaceName,
   71|     23|    ) -> Result<Arc<dyn Interface>> {
   72|     23|        let list = self
                          ^22
   73|     23|            .objects
   74|     23|            .get(path)
   75|     23|            .ok_or_else(|| Error::UnknownObject { path: path.clone() })?;
                                                                      ^1   ^1      ^1^1
   76|     22|        list.iter()
   77|     22|            .find(|i| &i.name() == interface)
   78|     22|            .cloned()
   79|     22|            .ok_or_else(|| Error::UnknownInterface {
   80|      1|                path: path.clone(),
   81|      1|                interface: interface.clone(),
   82|      1|            })
   83|     23|    }
   84|       |
   85|       |    /// Resolve and invoke in one step.
   86|     23|    pub async fn dispatch(
   87|     23|        &self,
   88|     23|        path: &ObjectPath,
   89|     23|        interface: &InterfaceName,
   90|     23|        member: &MemberName,
   91|     23|        args: Value,
   92|     23|    ) -> Result<Value> {
   93|     23|        let target = self.lookup(path, interface)?;
                          ^21                                  ^2
   94|     21|        if !target.members().contains(member) {
   95|      3|            return Err(Error::UnknownMethod {
   96|      3|                interface: interface.clone(),
   97|      3|                member: member.clone(),
   98|      3|            });
   99|     18|        }
  100|     18|        target.call(member, args).await
  101|     21|    }
  102|       |}
  103|       |

    1|       |//! The in-process transport: a pair of bounded channels, no file descriptors.
    2|       |//!
    3|       |//! This is not a test double bolted on beside the real thing — it is a first
    4|       |//! class transport, and it earns its place twice over.
    5|       |//!
    6|       |//! 1. **The test suite.** A broker, several services and a client run inside
    7|       |//!    one `#[tokio::test]`, so a test can assert on exact message ordering
    8|       |//!    without a socket, a temp dir, or a sleep.
    9|       |//! 2. **The slim kernel build.** An OpenHuman build that compiles an
   10|       |//!    integration in-process (because it is cheap, or because the platform has
   11|       |//!    no sockets) uses the *same* bus, the same interfaces and the same proxy
   12|       |//!    code as the out-of-process one. Moving an integration across that line is
   13|       |//!    a deployment decision, not a rewrite.
   14|       |//!
   15|       |//! Channels are **bounded**. An unbounded channel would turn a slow service
   16|       |//! into unbounded kernel memory growth — the failure mode we are trying to
   17|       |//! delete, not relocate. When a peer's queue fills, the sender waits, which is
   18|       |//! backpressure a caller can observe and time out on.
   19|       |
   20|       |use std::sync::Arc;
   21|       |
   22|       |use async_trait::async_trait;
   23|       |use tokio::sync::{Mutex, mpsc};
   24|       |
   25|       |use crate::error::{Error, Result};
   26|       |use crate::message::Message;
   27|       |use crate::ports::{Listener, Transport};
   28|       |
   29|       |/// How many messages may sit in a peer's queue before senders wait.
   30|       |///
   31|       |/// Sized for burst absorption, not for buffering: a queue this deep already
   32|       |/// means the consumer is not keeping up, and the right answer then is
   33|       |/// backpressure rather than a longer queue.
   34|       |pub const CHANNEL_CAPACITY: usize = 256;
   35|       |
   36|       |/// One end of an in-process link.
   37|       |///
   38|       |/// The outbound sender lives in an `Option` behind a *std* mutex so that
   39|       |/// [`Transport::close`] can drop it: dropping the sender is what makes the far
   40|       |/// end's `recv` return `None`, and without that a closed in-process link would
   41|       |/// look, to the peer, exactly like an idle one. The lock is a std mutex rather
   42|       |/// than a tokio one precisely so that it is never held across the send await.
   43|       |pub struct MemoryTransport {
   44|       |    outbound: std::sync::Mutex<Option<mpsc::Sender<Message>>>,
   45|       |    inbound: Mutex<mpsc::Receiver<Message>>,
   46|       |    label: String,
   47|       |}
   48|       |
   49|       |impl MemoryTransport {
   50|       |    /// Build a connected pair. Whatever one end sends, the other receives.
   51|     78|    pub fn pair() -> (Self, Self) {
   52|     78|        let (a_tx, a_rx) = mpsc::channel(CHANNEL_CAPACITY);
   53|     78|        let (b_tx, b_rx) = mpsc::channel(CHANNEL_CAPACITY);
   54|     78|        (Self::new(a_tx, b_rx), Self::new(b_tx, a_rx))
   55|     78|    }
   56|       |
   57|    156|    fn new(outbound: mpsc::Sender<Message>, inbound: mpsc::Receiver<Message>) -> Self {
   58|    156|        Self {
   59|    156|            outbound: std::sync::Mutex::new(Some(outbound)),
   60|    156|            inbound: Mutex::new(inbound),
   61|    156|            label: "memory".to_string(),
   62|    156|        }
   63|    156|    }
   64|       |
   65|       |    /// Take a clone of the sender without holding the lock across an await.
   66|  1.16k|    fn sender(&self) -> Result<mpsc::Sender<Message>> {
   67|  1.16k|        self.outbound
   68|  1.16k|            .lock()
   69|  1.16k|            .expect("the outbound lock is never held across a panic point")
   70|  1.16k|            .clone()
   71|  1.16k|            .ok_or_else(|| Error::transport("this end of the in-process link is closed"))
                                         ^0
   72|  1.16k|    }
   73|       |}
   74|       |
   75|       |#[async_trait]
   76|       |impl Transport for MemoryTransport {
   77|  1.16k|    async fn send(&self, message: Message) -> Result<()> {
   78|       |        self.sender()?
   79|       |            .send(message)
   80|       |            .await
   81|      2|            .map_err(|_| Error::transport("the peer end of the in-process link was dropped"))
   82|  1.16k|    }
   83|       |
   84|    986|    async fn recv(&self) -> Result<Option<Message>> {
   85|       |        // Single-reader contract (see the port docs) makes holding this lock
   86|       |        // across the await safe: there is never a second task to block.
   87|       |        let mut inbound = self.inbound.lock().await;
   88|       |        Ok(inbound.recv().await)
   89|    986|    }
   90|       |
   91|      2|    async fn close(&self) -> Result<()> {
   92|       |        // Dropping the sender is the hangup the far end sees as `Ok(None)`.
   93|       |        // Idempotent: `take` on an already-empty slot is not an error, because
   94|       |        // a shutdown race closing twice is normal.
   95|       |        self.outbound
   96|       |            .lock()
   97|       |            .expect("the outbound lock is never held across a panic point")
   98|       |            .take();
   99|       |        Ok(())
  100|      2|    }
  101|       |
  102|      0|    fn describe(&self) -> String {
  103|      0|        self.label.clone()
  104|      0|    }
  105|       |}
  106|       |
  107|       |/// An in-process bus: a listener plus the connect side that feeds it.

/home/enamakel/work/tinybus/worktrees/zbus-coverage-90/crates/tinybus/src/transport/unix.rs:
    1|       |//! The production transport: length-prefixed JSON over a Unix domain socket.
    2|       |//!
    3|       |//! A socket, not TCP, and that is a security decision rather than a performance
    4|       |//! one. The bus is a capability handle: anything that can connect can ask the
    5|       |//! wallet service to sign, or the mail service to read. A Unix socket inherits
    6|       |//! filesystem permissions, so `0700` on the containing directory is the whole
    7|       |//! access-control story; a TCP port has no such story and would need
    8|       |//! authentication invented on top. That is also why the socket lives under the
    9|       |//! user's runtime directory and not `/tmp`, which is world-writable.
   10|       |//!
   11|       |//! Windows gets a named-pipe backend as a sibling module, not as `#[cfg]`
   12|       |//! sprinkled through this one. See `ROADMAP.md`.
   13|       |
   14|       |use std::path::{Path, PathBuf};
   15|       |
   16|       |use async_trait::async_trait;
   17|       |use tokio::io::{AsyncReadExt, AsyncWriteExt};
   18|       |use tokio::net::{UnixListener, UnixStream};
   19|       |use tokio::sync::Mutex;
   20|       |
   21|       |use crate::error::{Error, Result};
   22|       |use crate::message::Message;
   23|       |use crate::message::codec::{self, LENGTH_PREFIX_LEN};
   24|       |use crate::ports::{Listener, Transport};
   25|       |
   26|       |/// One peer's socket, split into an independently locked reader and writer.
   27|       |///
   28|       |/// Two locks rather than one: `send` is called concurrently by every proxy on
   29|       |/// the connection while the single reader task is parked in `recv`. Sharing one
   30|       |/// lock would make an idle read block every write.
   31|       |pub struct UnixTransport {
   32|       |    reader: Mutex<tokio::net::unix::OwnedReadHalf>,
   33|       |    writer: Mutex<tokio::net::unix::OwnedWriteHalf>,
   34|       |    label: String,
   35|       |}
   36|       |
   37|       |impl UnixTransport {
   38|       |    /// Wrap an already-connected stream.
   39|      4|    pub fn new(stream: UnixStream, label: impl Into<String>) -> Self {
   40|      4|        let (reader, writer) = stream.into_split();
   41|      4|        Self {
   42|      4|            reader: Mutex::new(reader),
   43|      4|            writer: Mutex::new(writer),
   44|      4|            label: label.into(),
   45|      4|        }
   46|      4|    }
   47|       |
   48|       |    /// Dial the broker at `path`.
   49|      2|    pub async fn connect(path: impl AsRef<Path>) -> Result<Self> {
   50|      2|        let path = path.as_ref();
   51|      2|        let stream = UnixStream::connect(path)
   52|      2|            .await
   53|      2|            .map_err(|e| Error::path(path, format!("could not connect to the bus: {e}")))?;
                                       ^0          ^0    ^0                                            ^0
   54|      2|        Ok(Self::new(stream, format!("unix:{}", path.display())))
   55|      2|    }
   56|       |}
   57|       |
   58|       |#[async_trait]
   59|       |impl Transport for UnixTransport {
   60|      1|    async fn send(&self, message: Message) -> Result<()> {
   61|       |        let frame = codec::encode(&message)?;
   62|       |        let mut writer = self.writer.lock().await;
   63|       |        // One `write_all` for the whole frame: a length prefix written
   64|       |        // separately from its payload can interleave with a concurrent sender
   65|       |        // and desynchronise the stream permanently.
   66|       |        writer.write_all(&frame).await?;
   67|       |        writer.flush().await?;
   68|       |        Ok(())
   69|      1|    }
   70|       |
   71|      2|    async fn recv(&self) -> Result<Option<Message>> {
   72|       |        let mut reader = self.reader.lock().await;
   73|       |        let mut prefix = [0u8; LENGTH_PREFIX_LEN];
   74|       |        match reader.read_exact(&mut prefix).await {
   75|       |            Ok(_) => {}
   76|       |            // A clean hangup on a frame boundary is a peer exiting normally.
   77|       |            Err(e) if e.kind() == std::io::ErrorKind::UnexpectedEof => return Ok(None),
   78|       |            Err(e) => return Err(e.into()),
   79|       |        }
   80|       |        let len = codec::decode_length(prefix)?;
   81|       |        let mut payload = vec![0u8; len];
   82|      0|        reader.read_exact(&mut payload).await.map_err(|e| {
   83|       |            // Mid-frame EOF is not a clean shutdown: the peer died holding a
   84|       |            // half-written message, and the caller waiting on a reply needs to
   85|       |            // learn that rather than see a tidy `None`.
   86|      0|            if e.kind() == std::io::ErrorKind::UnexpectedEof {
   87|      0|                Error::protocol("peer disappeared mid-frame")
   88|       |            } else {
   89|      0|                Error::Io(e)
   90|       |            }
   91|      0|        })?;
   92|       |        Ok(Some(codec::decode(&payload)?))
   93|      2|    }
   94|       |
   95|      0|    async fn close(&self) -> Result<()> {
   96|       |        let mut writer = self.writer.lock().await;
   97|       |        // `shutdown` on an already-closed socket is a normal shutdown race, so
   98|       |        // its error is swallowed rather than surfaced — see the port contract.
   99|       |        let _ = writer.shutdown().await;
  100|       |        Ok(())
  101|      0|    }
  102|       |
  103|      0|    fn describe(&self) -> String {
  104|      0|        self.label.clone()
  105|      0|    }
  106|       |}

/home/enamakel/work/tinybus/worktrees/zbus-coverage-90/crates/tinybus/src/version.rs:
    1|       |//! Peer version negotiation: who is on the bus, what they speak, what they accept.
    2|       |//!
    3|       |//! # The problem this solves
    4|       |//!
    5|       |//! Every peer on the bus is built separately. The kernel ships on its own
    6|       |//! cadence; an integration is a different repository with a different release
    7|       |//! schedule; a user may be running one of each from three months apart. Until
    8|       |//! now the only thing tinybus said about that was "rename the interface on a
    9|       |//! breaking change" — a rule with no enforcement, whose failure mode is a
   10|       |//! decode error at the first call, in the *caller's* logs, blaming JSON.
   11|       |//!
   12|       |//! So each peer declares a [`PeerManifest`]: its own identity and version, and
   13|       |//! for every interface it touches, the version it **speaks** and the range it
   14|       |//! **accepts** from others. The broker holds those manifests and hands them
   15|       |//! out, so a peer can find out whether it can talk to another peer *before*
   16|       |//! sending anything, and can say precisely why not when it cannot.
   17|       |//!
   18|       |//! ```text
   19|       |//! kernel                          voice service
   20|       |//!   speaks  Voice 2.1.0             speaks  Voice 2.3.0
   21|       |//!   accepts Voice >=2.0.0, <3.0.0   accepts Voice >=2.0.0, <3.0.0
   22|       |//!                    ↓ compatible both ways ↓
   23|       |//!
   24|       |//! kernel                          wallet service
   25|       |//!   speaks  Wallet 1.4.0            speaks  Wallet 2.0.0
   26|       |//!   accepts Wallet >=1.0.0, <2.0.0  accepts Wallet >=2.0.0, <3.0.0
   27|       |//!                    ↓ IncompatibleVersion, named at startup ↓
   28|       |//! ```
   29|       |//!
   30|       |//! # Why the check is two-sided
   31|       |//!
   32|       |//! It would be simpler to compare one version against one range. But the two
   33|       |//! peers are not symmetric in what they know: a caller can be newer than a
   34|       |//! service *or* older, and only the party that is behind knows what it cannot
   35|       |//! parse. Checking `a.speaks ∈ b.accepts` **and** `b.speaks ∈ a.accepts` is
   36|       |//! what catches both directions; checking one catches half the skew and gives
   37|       |//! false confidence about the rest.
   38|       |//!
   39|       |//! # Why a hand-rolled version type
   40|       |//!
   41|       |//! `semver` is a good crate and this is a deliberate subset of it: three
   42|       |//! numbers and a half-open range. In a project whose entire purpose is removing
   43|       |//! dependencies from a graph, adding one to compare three integers is the wrong
   44|       |//! trade — and the subset is not a simplification of convenience, it is the
   45|       |//! whole of what a range check on the bus needs. Pre-release and build metadata
   46|       |//! are parsed and preserved so a version string round-trips, but they are not
   47|       |//! ordered: a peer that needs to distinguish `2.0.0-rc1` from `2.0.0` is
   48|       |//! describing two interfaces, not one.
   49|       |
   50|       |use std::cmp::Ordering;
   51|       |use std::fmt;
   52|       |use std::str::FromStr;
   53|       |
   54|       |use serde::{Deserialize, Serialize};
   55|       |
   56|       |use crate::error::{Error, Result};
   57|       |use crate::name::{BusName, InterfaceName};
   58|       |
   59|       |/// A `major.minor.patch` version, with optional trailing metadata.

   61|       |#[serde(try_from = "String", into = "String")]

   70|       |    pub tag: Option<String>,
   71|       |}

  117|       |    /// The first version that breaks compatibility with this one.
  118|       |    ///
  119|       |    /// `0.x` treats every minor as breaking, which is the convention pre-1.0
  120|       |    /// crates actually follow.
  121|     27|    pub fn next_breaking(&self) -> Version {
  122|     27|        if self.major == 0 {
  123|      1|            Version::new(0, self.minor + 1, 0)
  124|       |        } else {
  125|     26|            Version::new(self.major + 1, 0, 0)
  126|       |        }
  127|     27|    }
  128|       |
  129|       |    /// The range a **consumer** of this version accepts: anything from `self`
  130|       |    /// up to the next breaking version.
  131|       |    ///
  132|       |    /// Lower-bounded at `self` because a caller written against 2.3 may use
  133|       |    /// something 2.1 does not implement. This is semver's caret rule.
  134|     15|    pub fn caret(&self) -> VersionRange {
  135|     15|        VersionRange {
  136|     15|            min: Version::new(self.major, self.minor, self.patch),
  137|     15|            max_exclusive: Some(self.next_breaking()),
  138|     15|        }
  139|     15|    }
  140|       |
  141|       |    /// The range a **provider** at this version accepts: the whole compatible
  142|       |    /// series, not just versions at or above its own.
  143|       |    ///
  144|       |    /// The asymmetry with [`Version::caret`] is the whole point, and getting it
  145|       |    /// wrong is the obvious mistake: minor versions are *additions*, so a
  146|       |    /// provider at 2.3 serves a caller written against 2.0 perfectly well — the
  147|       |    /// caller simply uses less of it. Defaulting a provider to caret would
  148|       |    /// reject every client older than itself, which is every client, one
  149|       |    /// release later.
  150|     12|    pub fn compatible_series(&self) -> VersionRange {
  151|     12|        let min = if self.major == 0 {
  152|      0|            Version::new(0, self.minor, 0)
  153|       |        } else {
  154|     12|            Version::new(self.major, 0, 0)
  155|       |        };
  156|     12|        VersionRange {
  157|     12|            min,
  158|     12|            max_exclusive: Some(self.next_breaking()),
  159|     12|        }
  160|     12|    }
  161|       |}

  408|       |/// The result of checking one peer against another for one interface.
  409|       |#[derive(Debug, Clone, PartialEq, Eq)]
  410|       |pub enum Compatibility {
  411|       |    /// Both sides accept the other's version.
  412|       |    Compatible {
  413|       |        /// What the provider speaks.
  414|       |        provider_speaks: Version,
  415|       |        /// What the consumer speaks.
  416|       |        consumer_speaks: Version,
  417|       |    },
  418|       |    /// The provider speaks a version the consumer will not accept.
  419|       |    ConsumerRejects {
  420|       |        /// What the provider speaks.
  421|       |        provider_speaks: Version,
  422|       |        /// What the consumer will take.
  423|       |        consumer_accepts: VersionRange,
  424|       |    },
  425|       |    /// The consumer speaks a version the provider will not accept.
  426|       |    ProviderRejects {
  427|       |        /// What the consumer speaks.
  428|       |        consumer_speaks: Version,
  429|       |        /// What the provider will take.
  430|       |        provider_accepts: VersionRange,
  431|       |    },
  432|       |    /// The provider does not declare the interface at all.
  433|       |    NotProvided,
  434|       |}

  436|       |impl Compatibility {
  437|       |    /// Whether the two peers can talk.
  438|      7|    pub fn is_compatible(&self) -> bool {

  443|       |impl fmt::Display for Compatibility {
  444|      4|    fn fmt(&self, f: &mut fmt::Formatter<'_>) -> fmt::Result {
  445|      4|        match self {
  446|       |            Self::Compatible {
  447|      0|                provider_speaks,
  448|      0|                consumer_speaks,
  449|      0|            } => write!(
  450|      0|                f,
  451|       |                "compatible (provider {provider_speaks}, consumer {consumer_speaks})"
  452|       |            ),
  453|       |            Self::ConsumerRejects {
  454|      3|                provider_speaks,
  455|      3|                consumer_accepts,
  456|      3|            } => write!(
  457|      3|                f,
  458|       |                "the provider speaks {provider_speaks}, which the caller does not accept ({consumer_accepts})"
  459|       |            ),
  460|       |            Self::ProviderRejects {
  461|      1|                consumer_speaks,
  462|      1|                provider_accepts,
  463|      1|            } => write!(
  464|      1|                f,
  465|       |                "the caller speaks {consumer_speaks}, which the provider does not accept ({provider_accepts})"
  466|       |            ),
  467|      0|            Self::NotProvided => write!(f, "the peer does not declare this interface"),
  468|       |        }
  469|      4|    }
  470|       |}
  471|       |
  472|       |/// Check whether `consumer` can call `interface` on `provider`.
  473|       |///
  474|       |/// Both directions are checked; see the module docs for why one is not enough.
  475|       |/// A consumer that declares nothing for the interface is treated as accepting
  476|       |/// anything, so adding manifests to an existing bus is incremental rather than
  477|       |/// a flag day.
  478|     10|pub fn check(
  479|     10|    provider: &PeerManifest,
  480|     10|    consumer: &PeerManifest,
  481|     10|    interface: &InterfaceName,
  482|     10|) -> Compatibility {
  483|     10|    let Some(provided) = provider.provided(interface) else {
                           ^9
  484|      1|        return Compatibility::NotProvided;
  485|       |    };
  486|      9|    let Some(consumed) = consumer.consumed(interface) else {
                           ^8
  487|      1|        return Compatibility::Compatible {
  488|      1|            provider_speaks: provided.speaks.clone(),
  489|      1|            consumer_speaks: provided.speaks.clone(),
  490|      1|        };
  491|       |    };
  492|       |
  493|      8|    if !consumed.accepts.accepts(&provided.speaks) {
  494|      4|        return Compatibility::ConsumerRejects {
  495|      4|            provider_speaks: provided.speaks.clone(),
  496|      4|            consumer_accepts: consumed.accepts.clone(),
  497|      4|        };
  498|      4|    }
  499|      4|    if !provided.accepts.accepts(&consumed.speaks) {
  500|      1|        return Compatibility::ProviderRejects {
  501|      1|            consumer_speaks: consumed.speaks.clone(),
  502|      1|            provider_accepts: provided.accepts.clone(),
  503|      1|        };
  504|      3|    }
  505|      3|    Compatibility::Compatible {
  506|      3|        provider_speaks: provided.speaks.clone(),
  507|      3|        consumer_speaks: consumed.speaks.clone(),
  508|      3|    }
  509|     10|}
  510|       |
  511|       |/// A manifest paired with the peer that published it.
  512|       |#[derive(Debug, Clone, PartialEq, Eq, Serialize, Deserialize)]
  513|       |pub struct PeerRecord {
  514|       |    /// The peer's unique name.
  515|       |    pub peer: BusName,
  516|       |    /// The well-known names it owns, at the time of the query.
  517|       |    #[serde(default)]
  518|       |    pub names: Vec<BusName>,
  519|       |    /// What it declared.
  520|       |    pub manifest: PeerManifest,
  521|       |}
